{aby_cav_1g}Oromo Horsemen
{aby_cav_1g_descr}Highland Chäwa Cavalry are hardy mounted warriors recruited from Ethiopia's mountainous interior. Excellent horsemen from an early age, they rely upon speed, local knowledge, and aggressive charges to defeat their enemies. While less heavily equipped than the Imperial Cavalry, their endurance makes them ideal for long campaigns across difficult terrain. Few cavalry are better suited to the Ethiopian highlands.
{aby_cav_1g_descr_short}African Cavalry

{aby_cav_2g}Highland Mounted Rifles
{aby_cav_2g_descr}The Highland Mounted Rifles are skilled horsemen accustomed to traversing Ethiopia's rugged mountain trails. Armed with Martini-Henry rifles rather than traditional lances, they specialise in rapid manoeuvre, reconnaissance, and harassing enemy formations from a distance. Their mobility allows them to strike unexpectedly before withdrawing into difficult country. They are invaluable scouts during long campaigns.
{aby_cav_2g_descr_short}Highland Mounted Rifles (Carbine, Mauser M1871)

{aby_crb_3g_descr_short}Mounted Näftäñña (Carbine, Mauser M1871)

{aceh_gold}Aceh Golden Warriors 
{aceh_gold_descr}Aceh Sultan's Golden Warriors. Much like the Marines, they were trained and organized in the style of European Line Infantry. Until about 1870, the unit would have actually been led into battle by an Ottoman Turkish Imperial army officer, who served not only as the unit commander, but also as a foreign advisor and representative of the Ottoman Sultan, to whom the Sultan of Aceh was officially subject. Just like with the Marines, their weapons would've been issued to them by the Sultan, who purchased them out of pocket from European weapons merchants.
{aceh_gold_descr_short}Aceh Golden Warriors 

{aceh_inf}Aceh Grenadiers
{aceh_inf_descr}Grenadiers are well trained line infantry to defend the sultanate of Aceh against foreign oppressors. They are the more elite troops of the Acehnese with better equipment and training, and possibly, even experience. During the Acehnese wars, the Acehnese acknowledged the fact that they were pretty much having outdated equipments and military technology, thus some reformations were made in order for the Acehnese to win the war in terms of tactics and military operations. Even though the reformations were being rushed, but the kingdom managed to finally creating these elite troops to field enough firepowers to support the other troops. Although some of the nobles and people are against the use of advanced firearms as it is seen as taboo by some perspectives, some nobles and sultans are in favor in this idea.
{aceh_inf_descr_short}Grenadiers

{aceh_marines}Acehnese Marines
{aceh_marines_descr}Aceh Sultan's Marines. These were an elite unit of soldiers in the Sultan's army, inspired by the British Royal Marines. They were trained by European foreign military advisors and organized in the style of European Line Infantry. Their weapons would've been issued to them by the Sultan, who purchased them out of pocket from Turkish (and later Dutch) weapons merchants.
{aceh_marines_descr_short}Acehnese Marines

{aceh_noble}Aceh Noblemen
{aceh_noble_descr}Aceh Noblemen (Rifle-Musket, Pattern 1851 Minie)  Acehnese Nobles who have been given formal marksmanship training. They are armed with valuable European rifles, in many cases stolen. On the battlefield they keep their distance from the enemy, utilizing the long range provided by their weapons to sit back and take pot shots at enemy troops. The men in this unit can be described as cowardly; any return fire will almost certainly cause them to lose the will to fight.
{aceh_noble_descr_short}Aceh Noblemen (Rifle-Musket, Pattern 1851 Minie)

{aceh_reg}Aceh Infantry
{aceh_reg_descr}Infantry in the Acehnese Sultan's army. They are armed with smoothbore muskets, which the Acehnese have had in large numbers since the 17th century. Some of these are antiques, some acquired from Chinese smugglers, some from Turkish or Arab merchants, and others still are more modern pieces acquired from Dutch traders. For melee they use the Klewang, an Indonesian sword that can be wielded effectively in one hand. This weapon is notably smaller than the Kris, and its use was far more common. The unit is mixed gender, and includes both male and female fighters. The Sultanate of Aceh historically allowed women to fight in their armed forces.
{aceh_reg_descr_short}Aceh Infantry

{aceh_warband}Malay Warband
{aceh_warband_descr}The Malay Warband is recruited from the fiercely independent warriors of the Malay Archipelago, whose generations of warfare have produced skilled fighters both on land and along the coast. Armed with traditional weapons and whatever firearms are available, they favour swift attacks, ambushes, and close-quarters combat. Their courage and intimate knowledge of the dense jungles make them formidable irregular troops. They proudly defend their homeland against foreign invaders.
{aceh_warband_descr_short}Fierce Malay warriors renowned for jungle warfare and determined close-range assaults.

{Afghan Javelinmen}Nursitani Javelinmen

{berdan_inf}United States Sharpshooters
{berdan_inf_milan}United States Sharpshooters
{berdan_inf_descr}The United States Sharpshooters are elite marksmen personally selected for their exceptional accuracy and battlefield discipline. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. {byz_150mm_descr_short}Heavy artillery that has a massive range and fires big projectiles, very useful for sieges.

{byz_5lb}Mortar
{byz_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{byz_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.

{byz_armstrong_descr}Old Iron Cannon firing a mixture of solid shot and modern explosive shells
{byz_armstrong_descr_short}Old Iron Cannon firing a mixture of solid shot and modern explosive shells

{byz_gatling}Gatling Gun
{byz_gatling_descr}"It occurred to me that if I could invent a machine which could by its rapidity of fire, enable one man to do as much battle duty as a hundred, that it would, to a large extent supersede the necessity of large armies, and consequently, exposure to battle and disease be greatly diminished" - Richard J. Gatling \n\nThe Gatling Gun was invented in 1861, during the American Civil War, by Richard Jordan Gatling (Hertford County, 1818 - New York City, 1903). It was the first completely reliable machinegun of the era, combining a good firing rate, accuracy and an easy way to reload it. It uses a cyclic multi-barrel system that rotates clockwise and facilitates the cooling, aslo synchronizing the firing-reloading sequence. \n\nRichard Gatling invented the weapon to minimize the disease casualties of the American Civil War, however, the Gatling Gun was barely used by the Union forces because of the fear caused by such devastating gun. The Union forces used them during the siege of Petersburg (June 1864 - April 1865), it was later accepted by the army in 1866 after a demonstration made by a representative. The major European powers purchased and vastly used Gatling Guns for their colonial expansions, being the British the most well known for using them during the Anglo-Ashanti Wars and the Anglo-Zulu War. During the Boshin War, the Imperial Forces of Japan used some Gatling Guns; they were also vastly used during the War of the Pacific by the Peruvian and Chilean forces, in both Navy and Army, being especially used in the battle of Alto de la Alianza (1880) and the battle of San Juan y Chorrillos (1881). Later during the Spanish-American War, the US forces used few Gatling Guns, but in an effective way, against the Spanish forces in the battle of San Juan Hill (1898); however, the jungle footpaths and other incovenients were a problem for them, and most of the US forces were already using modern 1895 Browning Machineguns. \n\nThe Gatling Gun was rapidly displaced by other modern machineguns, and the US Army declared them obsolete in 1911. However, later, the mechanism and systems of the Gatling Gun were revived and reused to invent Miniguns and the famous M61 Vulcano.
{byz_gatling_descr_short}Early Machineguns that deal massive damage to close formations and have decent range.

{byz_maxim}Maxim Gun
{byz_maxim_descr}The Maxim Gun was invented by Sir Hiram Stevens Maxim in 1884, with a modern recoil-operated firing system, it was called "the weapon most associated with imperial conquest" by the historian Martin Gilbert, since it was vastly used in the Scramble for Africa by the colonial powers. It inspired the developement of more machineguns, the Maxim featured a water cooling, that would maintain it's rate of fire far longer than other guns, it could fire 600 rounds per minute. However, it was heavy, and would need a group of men to rapidly recharge and pour water to maintain the firing rate. \n\nMaxim established his own company to produce his guns, financied by Albert Vickers. The first patents related to the developement of the Maxim gun were registered in June and July of 1883. The Maxim Gun saw first use during the Scramble for Africa, mainly for it's psychological effect on the enemy. It was used extensively during the First Matabele War in Rhodesia. It devastated entire Anted in Tachankas. After that, the Maxim's variafrican armies because of their obsolete charging tactics. The military authorities of the era rejected Machineguns because of their tendency to jam. However, the Maxim gun was a lot more reliable than its contemporaries. \n\nThe Maxim Gun saw problems because of the smoke that firing it caused, however, many people (including Hiram's brother, Hudson Maxim) developed smokeless powder to solve this problem. The British adopted them under the guidance of Sir Garnet Wolseley, he had a good reputation and love for innovative ideas. Maxim's design was also purchased and used by several European countries. Both Japanese and Russian forces used it during the Russo-Japanese War. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, most of the European countries adopted more modern machineguns; the British Vickers MG was an improved and redesigned Maxim, while the German Maschinengewehr 08 and the Russian Pulemyot Maxim were more direct copies of the original Maxim gun. The Maxim was also used during the Russian Civil War, some depictions include it being mounts stayed in service during WW2 and the first decades of the Cold War.
{byz_maxim_descr_short}Late Machinegun that improves the range and uses a new recoil-operated firing system, effective against large number of enemies.

Their versatility and heavy armament make them dangerous opponents during this transitional period. Few vessels can match their combination of endurance and firepower.

This innovation allowed them to engage enemies regardless of heading while presenting a smaller target in battle. Their combination of armour, firepower, and flexibility rapidly rendered earlier ironclads outdated. They paved the way for every major battleship that followed.

{csa_12lb}Muzzle-loading Rifled Cannon
{csa_12lb_descr}Generally the 12 pounder guns during the early years of the XIXth Century were muzzle-loading and easy to manouver, being the 12 pounder Armstrong Gun a revolutionary one that featured an easier way to reload it, the breech-loading system, that greatly increased the firing rate. The Withworth gun was used by the US and Confederate forces, but it was considered rare. The Canon obusier de 12 was a heavy howitzer developed in France in 1853 and saw a vast use during the American Civil War by both Union and Confederate armies. The Obusier de 12 was rapidly adopted because of their variation of ammunition; despite being muzzle-loading, it was light enough to be transported rapidly on the field, strong enough to destroy heavy fortifications almost a mile away, and versatile enough to fire shells, balls or canister. \n\nMost of the cannons of the Union and the Confederate forces were 12 pounder Napoleon Model 1857; the Union manufactured over 1100 cannons while the Confederacy manufactured over 600 cannons. It was the last cast bronze gun used by the American Army; in the battle of Gettysburg, 142 of the 360 Union cannons were Napoleons. \n\nThe 12 pounder Armstrong was invented in Britain by William G. Armstrong and it was the first breech-loading 12 pounder gun in the world, it saw use in New Zealand during the First and Second Taranaki War. It was used by both the British Army and the Royal Navy, however, William Armstrong received a lot of complaints about his guns, so he retired from service in 1862 and Britain returned to the use of muzzle-loaders. Another reason of the displacement of the Armstrong gun was the cost of maintainment and the hard training of the gunners. \n\nLater, the British adopted new breech-loading cannons in 1885, the Ordnance BL 12-pounder 7 cwt, used first by the Royal Field Artillery and the Royal Horse Artillery, however, it was hard to transport by horses because of it's weight, so in 1892 the Royal Horse Artillery received a new 12 pounder 6 cwt gun. These cannons were in service until 1895, at that point the 12-pounder 7 cwt became redundant. The Royal Navy adopted the QF 12-pounder 8 cwt gun in 1894, it was used in the early stages of the Second Boer War and in the West and East Africa campaigns of WW1. In WW1 the British Army adopted the QF 12-pounder 12 cwt AA Gun, it was introduced after the German invasion of Belgium and northern France, because the British expected air attacks; it was usually used to defend dockyards and river barges (in Mesopotamia).
{csa_12lb_descr_short}Muzzle-loading rifled cannons that have a good range and can inflict good damage against tight formations, they can also use grapeshot.

{csa_maxim}Maxim Gun
{csa_maxim_descr}The Maxim Gun was invented by Sir Hiram Stevens Maxim in 1884, with a modern recoil-operated firing system, it was called "the weapon most associated with imperial conquest" by the historian Martin Gilbert, since it was vastly used in the Scramble for Africa by the colonial powers. It inspired the developement of more machineguns, the Maxim featured a water cooling, that would maintain it's rate of fire far longer than other guns, it could fire 600 rounds per minute. However, it was heavy, and would need a group of men to rapidly recharge and pour water to maintain the firing rate. \n\nMaxim established his own company to produce his guns, financied by Albert Vickers. The first patents related to the developement of the Maxim gun were registered in June and July of 1883. The Maxim Gun saw first use during the Scramble for Africa, mainly for it's psychological effect on the enemy. It was used extensively during the First Matabele War in Rhodesia. It devastated entire African armies because of their obsolete charging tactics. The military authorities of the era rejected Machineguns because of their tendency to jam. However, the Maxim gun was a lot more reliable than its contemporaries. \n\nThe Maxim Gun saw problems because of the smoke that firing it caused, however, many people (including Hiram's brother, Hudson Maxim) developed smokeless powder to solve this problem. The British adopted them under the guidance of Sir Garnet Wolseley, he had a good reputation and love for innovative ideas. Maxim's design was also purchased and used by several European countries. Both Japanese and Russian forces used it during the Russo-Japanese War. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, most of the European countries adopted more modern machineguns; the British Vickers MG was an improved and redesigned Maxim, while the German Maschinengewehr 08 and the Russian Pulemyot Maxim were more direct copies of the original Maxim gun. The Maxim was also used during the Russian Civil War, some depictions include it being mounted in Tachankas. After that, the Maxim's variants stayed in service during WW2 and the first decades of the Cold War.
{csa_maxim_descr_short}Late Machinegun that improves the range and uses a new recoil-operated firing system, effective against large number of enemies.

{eng_12lb}Muzzle-loading Rifled Cannon
{eng_12lb_descr}Generally the 12 pounder guns during the early years of the XIXth Century were muzzle-loading and easy to manouver, being the 12 pounder Armstrong Gun a revolutionary one that featured an easier way to reload it, the breech-loading system, that greatly increased the firing rate. The Withworth gun was used by the US and Confederate forces, but it was considered rare. The Canon obusier de 12 was a heavy howitzer developed in France in 1853 and saw a vast use during the American Civil War by both Union and Confederate armies. The Obusier de 12 was rapidly adopted because of their variation of ammunition; despite being muzzle-loading, it was light enough to be transported rapidly on the field, strong enough to destroy heavy fortifications almost a mile away, and versatile enough to fire shells, balls or canister. \n\nMost of the cannons of the Union and the Confederate forces were 12 pounder Napoleon Model 1857; the Union manufactured over 1100 cannons while the Confederacy manufactured over 600 cannons. It was the last cast bronze gun used by the American Army; in the battle of Gettysburg, 142 of the 360 Union cannons were Napoleons. \n\nThe 12 pounder Armstrong was invented in Britain by William G. Armstrong and it was the first breech-loading 12 pounder gun in the world, it saw use in New Zealand during the First and Second Taranaki War. It was used by both the British Army and the Royal Navy, however, William Armstrong received a lot of complaints about his guns, so he retired from service in 1862 and Britain returned to the use of muzzle-loaders. Another reason of the displacement of the Armstrong gun was the cost of maintainment and the hard training of the gunners. \n\nLater, the British adopted new breech-loading cannons in 1885, the Ordnance BL 12-pounder 7 cwt, used first by the Royal Field Artillery and the Royal Horse Artillery, however, it was hard to transport by horses because of it's weight, so in 1892 the Royal Horse Artillery received a new 12 pounder 6 cwt gun. These cannons were in service until 1895, at that point the 12-pounder 7 cwt became redundant. The Royal Navy adopted the QF 12-pounder 8 cwt gun in 1894, it was used in the early stages of the Second Boer War and in the West and East Africa campaigns of WW1. In WW1 the British Army adopted the QF 12-pounder 12 cwt AA Gun, it was introduced after the German invasion of Belgium and northern France, because the British expected air attacks; it was usually used to defend dockyards and river barges (in Mesopotamia).
{eng_12lb_descr_short}Muzzle-loading rifled cannons that have a good range and can inflict good damage against tight formations, they can also use grapeshot.

{eng_150mm}Howitzers 
{eng_150mm_descr}The 150 MM Guns (15 cm) have a ton of variations, being used by many countries around the world. These guns have a very high range compared to other artillery and fire bigger shells than regular artillery, serving for siege and defensive purposes mainly. The Germans developed the 15cm Kanone 16, used during WW1 and WW2, it would fire 2 types of highly explosive shells and served mainly for coastal defense during WW2. The 15cm L/40 Feldkanone also appeared in WW1, the Germans were desperate for long-range artillery, so they designed this new heavy artillery, it was mainly used in rails and mounted in platforms, however, land versions existed. The Italians used the Cannone da 149/23, designed and built by Gio. Ansaldo & C. It was introduced in 1882 and lasted until 1918, it's main purpose was aid during sieges and also serve as coastal artillery, being mounted in steel gun turrets. \n\nLater, the Italians introduced the Obice da 149/12, produced by Ansaldo and Vickers Terni, it entered service in 1914, having 3 different versions during the service in WW1, all of them being widely used by the Italian Army. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. The French introduced the Mortier de 150 mm T Mle 1917 during the last years of WW1, it was used until 1940, some of them participated during the Phony War, for transport it was broken down into three loads. \n\nIn the East, the Japanese designed the Type 89 15cm cannon, being the main gun of their heavy artillery units. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. The Wehrmatch used the 15 cm sFH 18 as their main howitzer, it had great success and was the first artillery that introduced rocket-assisted ammunition, replacing th
{eng_150mm_descr_short}Heavy artillery that has a massive range and fires big projectiles, very useful for sieges.

{eng_5lb}Mortar
{eng_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{eng_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.

{eng_armstrong}Breech-Loading Rifled Cannon
{eng_armstrong_descr}Breech-Loading Rifled Cannon
{eng_armstrong_descr_short}Breech-Loading Rifled Cannon

{eng_gatling}Gatling Gun 
{eng_gatling_descr}"It occurred to me that if I could invent a machine which could by its rapidity of fire, enable one man to do as much battle duty as a hundred, that it would, to a large extent supersede the necessity of large armies, and consequently, exposure to battle and disease be greatly diminished" - Richard J. Gatling \n\nThe Gatling Gun was invented in 1861, during the American Civil War, by Richard Jordan Gatling (Hertford County, 1818 - New York City, 1903). It was the first completely reliable machinegun of the era, combining a good firing rate, accuracy and an easy way to reload it. It uses a cyclic multi-barrel system that rotates clockwise and facilitates the cooling, aslo synchronizing the firing-reloading sequence. \n\nRichard Gatling invented the weapon to minimize the disease casualties of the American Civil War, however, the Gatling Gun was barely used by the Union forces because of the fear caused by such devastating gun. The Union forces used them during the siege of Petersburg (June 1864 - April 1865), it was later accepted by the army in 1866 after a demonstration made by a representative. The major European powers purchased and vastly used Gatling Guns for their colonial expansions, being the British the most well known for using them during the Anglo-Ashanti Wars and the Anglo-Zulu War. During the Boshin War, the Imperial Forces of Japan used some Gatling Guns; they were also vastly used during the War of the Pacific by the Peruvian and Chilean forces, in both Navy and Army, being especially used in the battle of Alto de la Alianza (1880) and the battle of San Juan y Chorrillos (1881). Later during the Spanish-American War, the US forces used few Gatling Guns, but in an effective way, against the Spanish forces in the battle of San Juan Hill (1898); however, the jungle footpaths and other incovenients were a problem for them, and most of the US forces were already using modern 1895 Browning Machineguns. \n\nThe Gatling Gun was rapidly displaced by other modern machineguns, and the US Army declared them obsolete in 1911. However, later, the mechanism and systems of the Gatling Gun were revived and reused to invent Miniguns and the famous M61 Vulcano.
{eng_gatling_descr_short}Early Machineguns that deal massive damage to close formations and have decent range.

{fr_chasseur}Chasseurs à Pied

{fra_12lb}Muzzle-loading Rifled Cannon
{fra_12lb_descr}Generally the 12 pounder guns during the early years of the XIXth Century were muzzle-loading and easy to manouver, being the 12 pounder Armstrong Gun a revolutionary one that featured an easier way to reload it, the breech-loading system, that greatly increased the firing rate. The Withworth gun was used by the US and Confederate forces, but it was considered rare. The Canon obusier de 12 was a heavy howitzer developed in France in 1853 and saw a vast use during the American Civil War by both Union and Confederate armies. The Obusier de 12 was rapidly adopted because of their variation of ammunition; despite being muzzle-loading, it was light enough to be transported rapidly on the field, strong enough to destroy heavy fortifications almost a mile away, and versatile enough to fire shells, balls or canister. \n\nMost of the cannons of the Union and the Confederate forces were 12 pounder Napoleon Model 1857; the Union manufactured over 1100 cannons while the Confederacy manufactured over 600 cannons. It was the last cast bronze gun used by the American Army; in the battle of Gettysburg, 142 of the 360 Union cannons were Napoleons. \n\nThe 12 pounder Armstrong was invented in Britain by William G. Armstrong and it was the first breech-loading 12 pounder gun in the world, it saw use in New Zealand during the First and Second Taranaki War. It was used by both the British Army and the Royal Navy, however, William Armstrong received a lot of complaints about his guns, so he retired from service in 1862 and Britain returned to the use of muzzle-loaders. Another reason of the displacement of the Armstrong gun was the cost of maintainment and the hard training of the gunners. \n\nLater, the British adopted new breech-loading cannons in 1885, the Ordnance BL 12-pounder 7 cwt, used first by the Royal Field Artillery and the Royal Horse Artillery, however, it was hard to transport by horses because of it's weight, so in 1892 the Royal Horse Artillery received a new 12 pounder 6 cwt gun. These cannons were in service until 1895, at that point the 12-pounder 7 cwt became redundant. The Royal Navy adopted the QF 12-pounder 8 cwt gun in 1894, it was used in the early stages of the Second Boer War and in the West and East Africa campaigns of WW1. In WW1 the British Army adopted the QF 12-pounder 12 cwt AA Gun, it was introduced after the German invasion of Belgium and northern France, because the British expected air attacks; it was usually used to defend dockyards and river barges (in Mesopotamia).
{fra_12lb_descr_short}Muzzle-loading rifled cannons that have a good range and can inflict good damage against tight formations, they can also use grapeshot.

{fra_150mm}Howitzers
{fra_150mm_descr}The 150 MM Guns (15 cm) have a ton of variations, being used by many countries around the world. These guns have a very high range compared to other artillery and fire bigger shells than regular artillery, serving for siege and defensive purposes mainly. The Germans developed the 15cm Kanone 16, used during WW1 and WW2, it would fire 2 types of highly explosive shells and served mainly for coastal defense during WW2. The 15cm L/40 Feldkanone also appeared in WW1, the Germans were desperate for long-range artillery, so they designed this new heavy artillery, it was mainly used in rails and mounted in platforms, however, land versions existed. The Italians used the Cannone da 149/23, designed and built by Gio. Ansaldo & C. It was introduced in 1882 and lasted until 1918, it's main purpose was aid during sieges and also serve as coastal artillery, being mounted in steel gun turrets. \n\nLater, the Italians introduced the Obice da 149/12, produced by Ansaldo and Vickers Terni, it entered service in 1914, having 3 different versions during the service in WW1, all of them being widely used by the Italian Army. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. The French introduced the Mortier de 150 mm T Mle 1917 during the last years of WW1, it was used until 1940, some of them participated during the Phony War, for transport it was broken down into three loads. \n\nIn the East, the Japanese designed the Type 89 15cm cannon, being the main gun of their heavy artillery units. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. 
{fra_150mm_descr_short}Heavy artillery that has a massive range and fires big projectiles, very useful for sieges.

{fra_5lb}Mortar
{fra_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{fra_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.

{fra_gatling}Mitrailleuse
{fra_gatling_descr}A mitrailleuse is a type of volley gun with barrels of rifle calibre that can fire either all rounds at once or in rapid succession. The earliest true mitrailleuse was invented in 1851 by Belgian Army captain Fafschamps, ten years before the advent of the Gatling gun. It was followed by the Belgian Montigny mitrailleuse in 1863. Then the French 25 barrel "Canon à Balles", better known as the Reffye mitrailleuse, was adopted in great secrecy in 1866. It became the first rapid-firing weapon deployed as standard equipment by any army in a major conflict when it was used during the Franco-Prussian War of 1870–71.
{fra_gatling_descr_short}Early Machineguns that deal massive damage to close formations and have decent range.

{fra_guard_ve}Carabinieri
{fra_guard_ve_descr}The Carabinieri were basically the Italian version of what the Grenzers were in this era, a sort of specialized mountain region Militia, and in Italy's case they operated exclusively around the Italian Alps. Whereas the Grenzers were used by the Austro-Hungarian Empire predominantly for the purposes of policing and squashing dissent, the Italian Carabinieri were proper border guards, and most of what they did in this era was to arrest spies and smugglers. They were soldiers, however, and they assisted the regular Italian military during the war against France in 1859 and again in the war against Austria in 1866. As their name implies, they were traditionally armed with carbines, which, although lacking the range and accuracy of the long rifles, were easier to carry and could be reloaded more quickly. In 1870 Italy adopted the Vetterli rifle, and the breech loading mechanism meant that the long rifles could be reloaded just as quickly and easily as the carbines. This nullified the greatest advantage of the carbine for these units, and after some discussion the Italian arms board decided to re equip the Carabinieri with the Vetterli long rifles in 1874.
{fra_guard_ve_descr_short}Light Infantry

{fra_rec}Garde Nationale
{fra_rec_descr}Napoleon III confined the National Guard during the Second Empire to subordinate tasks to reduce its liberal and republican influence. During the Franco-Prussian War the Government of National Defense of 1870 called on the Guard to undertake a major role in defending Paris against the invading Prussian army. During the uprising of the Paris Commune, from March to May 1871, the National Guard in Paris was expanded to include all able-bodied citizens capable of carrying weapons. Following the Commune's defeat by the regular French Army, the National Guard was officially abolished and its units disbanded. Also disbanded was the Mobile National Guard (Garde Nationale Mobile) raised in 1866 to provide personnel and officers for rapid deployment operations nationwide, as well as to provide reserve personnel for the armed forces.
{fra_rec_descr_short}Garde Nationale (Rifle-Musket, Fusil modèle 1857)

{ind_150mm}Howitzers

Slow to deploy and difficult to manoeuvre, it relies upon careful positioning and protection from the army it supports. When properly employed, few defences can withstand its bombardment.

{ind_5lb}Mortar
{ind_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{ind_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.

{ind_armstrong}Cast Bronze Cannon
{ind_armstrong_descr}Old Bronze Cannon firing solid shot
{ind_armstrong_descr_short}Old Bronze Cannon firing solid shot

{ind_gatling}Gatling Gun
{ind_gatling_descr}"It occurred to me that if I could invent a machine which could by its rapidity of fire, enable one man to do as much battle duty as a hundred, that it would, to a large extent supersede the necessity of large armies, and consequently, exposure to battle and disease be greatly diminished" - Richard J. Gatling \n\nThe Gatling Gun was invented in 1861, during the American Civil War, by Richard Jordan Gatling (Hertford County, 1818 - New York City, 1903). It was the first completely reliable machinegun of the era, combining a good firing rate, accuracy and an easy way to reload it. It uses a cyclic multi-barrel system that rotates clockwise and facilitates the cooling, aslo synchronizing the firing-reloading sequence. \n\nRichard Gatling invented the weapon to minimize the disease casualties of the American Civil War, however, the Gatling Gun was barely used by the Union forces because of the fear caused by such devastating gun. The Union forces used them during the siege of Petersburg (June 1864 - April 1865), it was later accepted by the army in 1866 after a demonstration made by a representative. The major European powers purchased and vastly used Gatling Guns for their colonial expansions, being the British the most well known for using them during the Anglo-Ashanti Wars and the Anglo-Zulu War. During the Boshin War, the Imperial Forces of Japan used some Gatling Guns; they were also vastly used during the War of the Pacific by the Peruvian and Chilean forces, in both Navy and Army, being especially used in the battle of Alto de la Alianza (1880) and the battle of San Juan y Chorrillos (1881). Later during the Spanish-American War, the US forces used few Gatling Guns, but in an effective way, against the Spanish forces in the battle of San Juan Hill (1898); however, the jungle footpaths and other incovenients were a problem for them, and most of the US forces were already using modern 1895 Browning Machineguns. \n\nThe Gatling Gun was rapidly displaced by other modern machineguns, and the US Army declared them obsolete in 1911. However, later, the mechanism and systems of the Gatling Gun were revived and reused to invent Miniguns and the famous M61 Vulcano.
{ind_gatling_descr_short}Early Machineguns that deal massive damage to close formations and have decent range.

{ind_maxim}Maxim Gun
{ind_maxim_descr}The Maxim Gun was invented by Sir Hiram Stevens Maxim in 1884, with a modern recoil-operated firing system, it was called "the weapon most associated with imperial conquest" by the historian Martin Gilbert, since it was vastly used in the Scramble for Africa by the colonial powers. It inspired the developement of more machineguns, the Maxim featured a water cooling, that would maintain it's rate of fire far longer than other guns, it could fire 600 rounds per minute. However, it was heavy, and would need a group of men to rapidly recharge and pour water to maintain the firing rate. \n\nMaxim established his own company to produce his guns, financied by Albert Vickers. The first patents related to the developement of the Maxim gun were registered in June and July of 1883. The Maxim Gun saw first use during the Scramble for Africa, mainly for it's psychological effect on the enemy. It was used extensively during the First Matabele War in Rhodesia. It devastated entire African armies because of their obsolete charging tactics. The military authorities of the era rejected Machineguns because of their tendency to jam. However, the Maxim gun was a lot more reliable than its contemporaries. \n\nThe Maxim Gun saw problems because of the smoke that firing it caused, however, many people (including Hiram's brother, Hudson Maxim) developed smokeless powder to solve this problem. The British adopted them under the guidance of Sir Garnet Wolseley, he had a good reputation and love for innovative ideas. Maxim's design was also purchased and used by several European countries. Both Japanese and Russian forces used it during the Russo-Japanese War. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, most of the European countries adopted more modern machineguns; the British Vickers MG was an improved and redesigned Maxim, while the German Maschinengewehr 08 and the Russian Pulemyot Maxim were more direct copies of the original Maxim gun. The Maxim was also used during the Russian Civil War, some depictions include it being mounted in Tachankas. After that, the Maxim's variants stayed in service during WW2 and the first decades of the Cold War.
{ind_maxim_descr_short}Late Machinegun that improves the range and uses a new recoil-operated firing system, effective against large number of enemies.

{jap_12lb}Muzzle-Loading Rifled Cannon
{jap_12lb_descr}Generally the 12 pounder guns during the early years of the XIXth Century were muzzle-loading and easy to manouver, being the 12 pounder Armstrong Gun a revolutionary one that featured an easier way to reload it, the breech-loading system, that greatly increased the firing rate. The Withworth gun was used by the US and Confederate forces, but it was considered rare. The Canon obusier de 12 was a heavy howitzer developed in France in 1853 and saw a vast use during the American Civil War by both Union and Confederate armies. The Obusier de 12 was rapidly adopted because of their variation of ammunition; despite being muzzle-loading, it was light enough to be transported rapidly on the field, strong enough to destroy heavy fortifications almost a mile away, and versatile enough to fire shells, balls or canister. \n\nMost of the cannons of the Union and the Confederate forces were 12 pounder Napoleon Model 1857; the Union manufactured over 1100 cannons while the Confederacy manufactured over 600 cannons. It was the last cast bronze gun used by the American Army; in the battle of Gettysburg, 142 of the 360 Union cannons were Napoleons. \n\nThe 12 pounder Armstrong was invented in Britain by William G. Armstrong and it was the first breech-loading 12 pounder gun in the world, it saw use in New Zealand during the First and Second Taranaki War. It was used by both the British Army and the Royal Navy, however, William Armstrong received a lot of complaints about his guns, so he retired from service in 1862 and Britain returned to the use of muzzle-loaders. Another reason of the displacement of the Armstrong gun was the cost of maintainment and the hard training of the gunners. \n\nLater, the British adopted new breech-loading cannons in 1885, the Ordnance BL 12-pounder 7 cwt, used first by the Royal Field Artillery and the Royal Horse Artillery, however, it was hard to transport by horses because of it's weight, so in 1892 the Royal Horse Artillery received a new 12 pounder 6 cwt gun. These cannons were in service until 1895, at that point the 12-pounder 7 cwt became redundant. The Royal Navy adopted the QF 12-pounder 8 cwt gun in 1894, it was used in the early stages of the Second Boer War and in the West and East Africa campaigns of WW1. In WW1 the British Army adopted the QF 12-pounder 12 cwt AA Gun, it was introduced after the German invasion of Belgium and northern France, because the British expected air attacks; it was usually used to defend dockyards and river barges (in Mesopotamia).
{jap_12lb_descr_short}Muzzle-loading rifled cannons that have a good range and can inflict good damage against tight formations, they can also use grapeshot.\nISHIN ONLY

{jap_150mm}Howitzers 
{jap_150mm_descr}The 150 MM Guns (15 cm) have a ton of variations, being used by many countries around the world. These guns have a very high range compared to other artillery and fire bigger shells than regular artillery, serving for siege and defensive purposes mainly. The Germans developed the 15cm Kanone 16, used during WW1 and WW2, it would fire 2 types of highly explosive shells and served mainly for coastal defense during WW2. The 15cm L/40 Feldkanone also appeared in WW1, the Germans were desperate for long-range artillery, so they designed this new heavy artillery, it was mainly used in rails and mounted in platforms, however, land versions existed. The Italians used the Cannone da 149/23, designed and built by Gio. Ansaldo & C. It was introduced in 1882 and lasted until 1918, it's main purpose was aid during sieges and also serve as coastal artillery, being mounted in steel gun turrets. \n\nLater, the Italians introduced the Obice da 149/12, produced by Ansaldo and Vickers Terni, it entered service in 1914, having 3 different versions during the service in WW1, all of them being widely used by the Italian Army. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. The French introduced the Mortier de 150 mm T Mle 1917 during the last years of WW1, it was used until 1940, some of them participated during the Phony War, for transport it was broken down into three loads. \n\nIn the East, the Japanese designed the Type 89 15cm cannon, being the main gun of their heavy artillery units. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. 
{jap_150mm_descr_short}Heavy artillery that has a massive range and fires big projectiles, very useful for sieges.\nISHIN ONLY

{jap_guard}Rikuguntai (1890s)
{jap_guard_descr}The Rikuguntai form the core of Japan's rapidly modernising Imperial Army, reflecting decades of military reform inspired by European powers. Armed with the dependable Type 22 Murata rifle and trained in modern battlefield tactics, they combine disciplined firepower with the determination that has become synonymous with Japan's new national army. Their professionalism continues to improve as the Empire emerges as a major military power. They are the backbone of Imperial Japan's infantry.
{jap_guard_descr_short}Professional Imperial Japanese infantry equipped with Murata rifles and trained in modern European doctrine.

{jap_hussar}Japanese Dragoons
{jap_hussar_descr}Japanese Dragoons provide the Imperial Army with fast-moving cavalry capable of scouting, screening, and pursuing broken enemies. Armed with Spencer repeating carbines and sabres, they combine impressive firepower with exceptional battlefield mobility. Their versatility allows them to fight effectively both mounted and on foot whenever circumstances demand. They represent Japan's successful adaptation of Western cavalry doctrine.
{jap_hussar_descr_short}Versatile Japanese cavalry armed with Spencer carbines for reconnaissance and rapid battlefield manoeuvre.

{jap_kami}Koheitai
{jap_kami_descr}The Koheitai are Japan's military engineers, trained to support advancing armies through technical skill rather than brute strength. Equipped with demolition charges, entrenching tools, and firearms for self-defence, they construct bridges, prepare field fortifications, and breach enemy obstacles under fire. Their specialised expertise allows the army to overcome positions that would otherwise halt an advance. No modern campaign can succeed without their invaluable support.
{jap_kami_descr_short}Military engineers trained to construct fortifications, breach obstacles, and support siege operations.

{jap_maxim}Maxim Gun
{jap_maxim_descr}The Maxim Gun was invented by Sir Hiram Stevens Maxim in 1884, with a modern recoil-operated firing system, it was called "the weapon most associated with imperial conquest" by the historian Martin Gilbert, since it was vastly used in the Scramble for Africa by the colonial powers. It inspired the developement of more machineguns, the Maxim featured a water cooling, that would maintain it's rate of fire far longer than other guns, it could fire 600 rounds per minute. However, it was heavy, and would need a group of men to rapidly recharge and pour water to maintain the firing rate. \n\nMaxim established his own company to produce his guns, financied by Albert Vickers. The first patents related to the developement of the Maxim gun were registered in June and July of 1883. The Maxim Gun saw first use during the Scramble for Africa, mainly for it's psychological effect on the enemy. It was used extensively during the First Matabele War in Rhodesia. It devastated entire African armies because of their obsolete charging tactics. The military authorities of the era rejected Machineguns because of their tendency to jam. However, the Maxim gun was a lot more reliable than its contemporaries. \n\nThe Maxim Gun saw problems because of the smoke that firing it caused, however, many people (including Hiram's brother, Hudson Maxim) developed smokeless powder to solve this problem. The British adopted them under the guidance of Sir Garnet Wolseley, he had a good reputation and love for innovative ideas. Maxim's design was also purchased and used by several European countries. Both Japanese and Russian forces used it during the Russo-Japanese War. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, most of the European countries adopted more modern machineguns; the British Vickers MG was an improved and redesigned Maxim, while the German Maschinengewehr 08 and the Russian Pulemyot Maxim were more direct copies of the original Maxim gun. The Maxim was also used during the Russian Civil War, some depictions include it being mounted in Tachankas. After that, the Maxim's variants stayed in service during WW2 and the first decades of the Cold War.
{jap_maxim_descr_short}Late Machinegun that improves the range and uses a new recoil-operated firing system, effective against large number of enemies.\nISHIN ONLY

{jap_ninja}Battoutai
{jap_ninja_descr}The battotai, or “Sword Corps,” were first formed during the Satsuma rebellion, as the regular imperial infantrymen were hopelessly outmatched in hand-to-hand combat by the samurai rebels. Many of the original battotai were former samurai themselves, and thus were skilled swordsmen.
{jap_ninja_descr_short}Battoutai

{Japan_Battotai}Battōtai
{Japan_Battotai_descr}Era: 1870s\nQuality: Elite\nPrimary: Tachi (sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan (Metropolitan Police)\n\nTest\n\n"On March 14, during the Battle of Tabaruzaka, our forces advanced toward a rebel stronghold. We tried to take it, but thirteen rebel soldiers firmly held their ground. Just then, a former Aizu soldier put himself in bodily danger and charged forward, immediately cutting down the thirteen rebels. As he slashed about, he called out loudly, 'Remember the Boshin, remember the Boshin!' This may sound like a work of fiction but it is by no means made up. It is said that this Aizu man suffered only slight wounds."\n-Inukai Tsuyoshi, Military journalist during the Satsuma Rebellion, Prime Minister of Japan\n\nThroughout the Satsuma Rebellion of 1877, the Imperial Japanese Army, though disciplined and well-motivated, were only conscripts and lacked the years of training their samurai foes possessed. This was most apparent in close combat, where they would suffer heavy losses. During the Battle of Tabaruzaka, the Satsuma rebels dug extensive trench networks along the hill, and their fortifications held strong in the face of repeated assaults. In order to relieve the besieged garrison of Kumamoto castle, a quick breakthrough was necessary. To accomplish this, a specialized corps of the Metropolitan Police was created. They were the battōtai ("Bare sword squad"), former samurai who were fully confident in their swordsmanship and volunteered to lead the charge into the rebel lines. The battōtai took heavy casualties, with over half the unit killed or wounded, but were ultimately successful. The heroic deeds of the battōtai are credited with the revival of Japanese swordsmanship, which had nearly died out after the fall of the Tokugawa shōgunate. A military march was composed in their honour, and was first performed at the Rokumeikan banquet hall later that year.
{Japan_Battotai_descr_short}Battōtai (Sword, Tachi)

{Japan_Bodyguard_1860}Japanese General and Umamawari
{Japan_Bodyguard_1860_descr}Era: 1860s\nQuality: Elite\nPrimary: Smith & Wesson No.2 (Revolver)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan & Tokugawa shōgunate\n\nTranslation: "Around the horse"\n\nThese high-ranking retainers accompany the army's general onto the battlefield. They are armed with handguns and swords and are perfectly respectable combatants, but are too few and valuable to do much fighting themselves. Rather, they are the last line of defence if the general's life is in danger.\n\nThe umamawari were the mounted samurai who accompanied their daimyō in battle, acting as both bodyguards and messengers. The position was established in the late 14th century to serve as the mounted guards of the Ashikaga shōguns, though it was not until the Sengoku jidai that they were widely adopted by the numerous daimyō across Japan. By the Edo period, it was common practice for the sons of direct shōgunate or domain vassals to begin their career in the umamawari, before their talents—and loyalty—were proven enough to qualify them as a higher-ranking official.
{Japan_Bodyguard_1860_descr_short}Japanese General and Umamawari (Revolver, Smith & Wesson No.2 and katana)

{Japan_Byakkotai}Aizu Byakkotai
{Japan_Byakkotai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Militia\nPrimary: Imported "geberu" (Smoothbore musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Aizu-han)\n\nTranslation: "White Tiger Force"\n\nThe byakkotai are the most basic line infantry available to the Tokugawa shōgunate beginning in 1865, though are only recruitable in the Tōhoku region. As expected of Aizu samurai, they possess strong morale for a unit of this role and can maintain a firing line for longer than one might expect. As they are poorly armed with smoothbore muskets and lack the swordsmanship skill of more experienced samurai, however, they cannot be relied on to kill many enemies.\n\n"No matter how many people wash the stones with their tears, these names will never vanish from the world."\n-Matsudaira Katmori, Daimyō of Aizu\n\nAizu domain was renowned for its samurai spirit throughout the Edo period, and this was true of its youth as well. After the Battle of Toba-Fushimi, the domain reformed its army following French doctrine, with all able-bodied samurai being organized into four age-based units. The byakkotai ("White Tiger Corps") were made up of those aged 16 to 17, though several 14 and 15 year olds lied about their age or were otherwise allowed to enlist as the situation in the Battle of Aizu grew more desperate. Most of the byakkotai fought from the battlements of Tsuruga Castle, but a detachment was sent to guard the supply lines at Tonoguchihara, with each of these soldiers being given a yellow armband from Matsudaira Katamori himself. In the chaos, a squad of 20 byakkotai were cut off from the rest of the Aizu army atop Mt. Iimori, where they could see flames emerging from houses within the castle town and assumed Tsuruga Castle itself was burning. Tragically, they believed the battle to be lost and chose to commit seppuku rather than surrender to the imperial army. Just one member, Iinuma Sadakichi, survived his attempt long enough to be rescued by a local farmer; it is only through his account that the story of his unit is known today. As enemies of the imperial army, their bodies were left exposed to the elements until permission for a proper burial was finally granted in 1872, with Katamori writing the poem inscribed on the monument. Sadakichi would later be buried with his comrades after his death in 1931.
{Japan_Byakkotai_descr_short}Aizu Byakkotai (Musket, Imported "geberu" and katana)\nTŌHOKU AOR\nSHŌGUNATE ONLY

{Japan_Denshutai}Denshūtai
{Japan_Denshutai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Elite\nPrimary: Fusil modèle 1866 Chassepot (Breech-loading rifle)\nSecondary: Bayonet\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Bakufu)\n\nThe elite denshūtai are available to the Tokugawa in the second half of the 1860s. Highly trained and armed with the revolutionary Chassepot Mle.1866, they are the finest line infantry not just in Japan, but the entire non-western world upon their introduction in the campaign. Even western units may find themselves outgunned by their state of the art weaponry!\n\n"A revolution is forcing the Military Mission to return to France. Alone I stay, alone I wish to continue, under new conditions: the results obtained by the Mission, together with the [Northern Alliance], which is the party favorable to France in Japan. Soon a reaction will take place, and the Daimyos of the North have offered me to be its soul."\n-Jules Brunet, Artillery advisor to the shōgunate forces\n\nFrance was eager to establish relations with the Tokugawa shōgunate, as they wanted to facilitate trade and to have a strong ally in east Asia. Tokugawa Yoshinobu highly respected the French and considered them to have the finest army in the west, resulting in a shift towards French-style doctrine and uniforms in the 1860s. A military mission was dispatched to Japan in 1867, including modern artillery and 2,000 cutting-edge Chassepot rifles as a personal gift from Napoleon III to Yoshinobu. The fifteen advisors would train an 800-strong elite corps known as the denshūtai. These men were educated in military strategy and even used French as the language of command, with the intention that they would become instructors in academies across Japan. However, the outbreak of the Boshin War the following year prevented this from coming to fruition, and the denshūtai would see extensive frontline combat over the course of the conflict. Five of their advisors, most famously lieutenant Jules Brunet, refused orders to return to France. They would fight alongside their men until the end of the war.
{Japan_Denshutai_descr_short}Denshūtai (Rifle, Fusil modèle 1866 Chassepot)\nKANTŌ AOR\nSHŌGUNATE ONLY

{Japan_Gakuheitai}Sendai Gakuheitai
{Japan_Gakuheitai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Elite\nPrimary: Snider-Enfield Mk.I (Breech-loading rifle)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Sendai-han)\n\nTranslation: "Western-style army"\n\n"Though the myriad domains have submitted petitions, [Aizu] has not been forgiven, and now a nameless order to attack Shōnai has been issued...The gang of [Satchō] villains has deceived the imperial court, stolen political power, and there is no doubt that they advance their private agenda with cunning and cruelty."\n-Date Yoshikuni, Daimyō of Sendai\n\nThe gakuheitai ("Western-style army") were an 800-strong elite infantry corps from Sendai domain, fully trained and equipped by British advisors. They were created after Sendai's forced entry into the Boshin War and only saw brief action in mainland Japan, but would regroup with other shogunate loyalists on Hokkaido, where they made up a significant portion of the Republic of Ezo's forces. They fought valiantly during the Battle of Hakodate, with most of the unit killed during the final battle of the war.

{Japan_Goshimpei_1870}Goshimpei (1870s)

{Japan_Heimin_Mob}Heimin Mob
{Japan_Heimin_Mob_descr}Era: 1860s\nQuality: Levy\nPrimary: Yari\nAllegiance: Locals / Mercenaries\n\nTranslation: "Common people"\n\nHeimin Mob.
{Japan_Heimin_Mob_descr_short}Heimin Mob (Spear, Yari)

{Japan_Heimin_Partisans}Heimin Partisans (Teppō)
{Japan_Heimin_Partisans_descr}Era: 1860s\nQuality: Levy\nPrimary: Tanegashima teppō (Arquebus)\nSecondary: Tantō (Dagger)\nAllegiance: Locals / Mercenaries\n\nTranslation: "Common people"\n\nWhether it be for an emergency or uprising, these peasants have taken up whatever weapons they can find for battle. Accustomed to using their muskets for hunting, while they lack the discipline or drill of proper soldiers, they can still certainly deliver lead downrange! These men are armed with a small tantō for hand-to-hand combat, though they would do well to avoid it at all costs.\n\n"The peasant is the foundation of the state and must be governed with care. He must be allowed neither too much, nor too little, but just enough rice to live on and keep for seed in the following year."\n-Honda Masanobu, Daimyō of Tamanawa\n\nDuring the Edo period, it was not uncommon for the heimin, or peasantry, to own weapons. While ownership of katana were restricted to samurai, shorter blades such as wakizashi and tanto were permitted to be owned and carried for self-defence. Firearms were also used for hunting, and with the turmoil of the 19th century, there were times where they were used in battle. The Chōshū domain saw the mobilization of peasant irregulars during the Shimonoseki campaign of 1863 to supplant their regular army. While the majority of combatants in the Boshin War were samurai, or at least commoners with military training, there were instances of peasants engaging in guerrilla warfare and participating in the defence of their homes as the forces of the new government advanced north.\n\nThe most dramatic instance of the peasantry taking up arms, however would occur in 1884. The economic hardship brought about by the new government's taxes and land reforms led to discontent across Japan, leading to the formation of a "Revolutionary Army" between 5,000 and 10,000 strong in Chichibu (near Tōkyō). They were met by the Imperial Japanese Army, who quickly crushed them with their newly adopted Type 13 Murata rifles. Likely to discredit the movement and to mask the severity of their military response, imperial officials downplayed the scale of the Chichibu incident, though it was one of the largest uprisings of the Meiji period.
{Japan_Heimin_Partisans_descr_short}Heimin Partisans (Arquebus, Tanegashima teppō and tantō)

{Japan_Hijikata_Toshizo}Hijikata Toshizō

{Japan_Hokokutai}Enshū Hōkokutai
{Japan_Hokokutai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Semi-elite\nPrimary: Geweer M.1848 (Rifled musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan (Hamamatsu-han)\n\nTranslation: "Enshū patriot corps"\n\nThese line infantry are made up of well-motivated volunteers, eager to fight in the emperor's name. They are well-armed with Dutch Minie rifles and have strong morale, making them a reliable component of any army.\n\n"I don't care about the way the world is governed, I'm happy to be an ordinary citizen."\n-Monument to the Enshū hōkokutai\n\nAfter the Battle of Toba-Fushimi, domains across Japan began to pledge allegiance to the new imperial government. In the city of Shizuoka, then part of Hamamatsu domain, a 300-man volunteer militia was formed to join the Satchō army. They were known as the hōkokutai (often referred to as the Enshū hōkokutai to differentiate them from a similarly named unit from Chōshū) and were largely made up of Shintō priests, funded by wealthy farmers and merchants from the Tōtōmi region. Their most notable combat action was during the Battle of Ueno, where they clashed with the shōgitai on the Imado Bridge. Afterwards, the unit split up; those who remained in Edo (now Tōkyō) set up a barracks near Nijubashi and were tasked with guarding the many bridges of Edo Castle, while the group that returned home guarded Hamamatsu's shōkonsha (a shrine to those killed in war). A monument commemorating the Enshū hōkokutai was later erected in Tōrimachi Park in 1907.
{Japan_Hokokutai_descr_short}Enshū Hōkokutai (Rifle-Musket, Geweer M.1848 and katana)\nKANSAI AOR\nISHIN ONLY

{Japan_Hoppo_Keibitai_1870}Hoppō Keibitai (1870s)
{Japan_Hoppo_Keibitai_1870_descr}Era: 1870s\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Fusil modèle 1866 Chassepot (Breech-loading rifle)\nSecondary: Bayonet\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Bakufu)\n\nTranslation: "Northern guard"\n\nTest\n\n"Here lies dandelions, and the graves of Aizu samurai."\n -Monument to the northern guard of Aizu domain\n\nSince the early Edo period, the northern islands of Ezo (the "barbaric north") were viewed with caution by the Tokugawa shōgunate. Hokkaidō was sparsely populated by the Japanese and home to the indigenous Ainu clans, who had revolted in the 17th and 18th centuries. By the 1800s, however, the more pressing threat was a possible Russian invasion, prompted by their settlements on Sakhalin and an intrusion by the Russian navy in 1804, during which a rogue envoy attempted to establish trade relations with Japan by force, even raiding and pillaging local fishing villages in the process. It was in this context that the Tokugawa shōgunate ordered the Tōhoku domains to organize an expedition to Ezo. Aizu answered the call, and in 1808 a force of 1,558 samurai, under the name hoppō keibitai, were dispatched. They set up their headquarters near Wakkanai and erected coastal batteries and fortifications on northern Hokkaidō, Rishiri Island, and southern Sakhalin. The Aizu expedition ultimately did not see combat, as the outbreak of the Napoleonic Wars in Europe led to Russian forces in the region being withdrawn, though they continued to contribute to Hokkaidō's defence even into the 1860s. Interestingly, the issue of Japan's northern frontier was acknowledged even during the Boshin War. The Tokugawa loyalists who fled to Ezo near the end of the conflict were neither fighting for independence or the restoration of the shōgunate, but rather the establishment of a new feudal domain for the Tokugawa clan, who would dedicate themselves to the "faithful guardianship of the northern gate."
{Japan_Hoppo_Keibitai_1870_descr_short}Hokuchin Butai (Rifle, Fusil modèle 1866 Chassepot)\nHOKKAIDŌ AOR\nSHŌGUNATE ONLY

{Japan_Itagaki_Taisuke}Itagaki Taisuke
{Japan_Itagaki_Taisuke_descr}Era: 1860s (1866)\nQuality: Elite\nPrimary: Spencer M1860 (Repeating carbine)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan (Tosa-han)\n\nTranslation: "Rapid assault force"\n\nHailing from Tosa domain, the jinshōtai are an elite light infantry force recruitable from 1866 onwards. Their Spencer M1860 repeating carbines grant them devastating firepower, and they are simultaneously the best swordsmen available to the Satchō army. These strengths are offset by a smaller unit size and high cost, and they are more a hybrid than true sharpshooters or shock troops.\n\n"The imperial army, fighting under the emperor's gold banner, must not engage in any conduct that would bring shame to the imperial chrysanthemum crest."\n-Itagaki Taisuke, Commander of the jinshōtai\n\nTosa was one of the most crucial members of the Satchō alliance. It was a samurai of Tosa, Sakamoto Ryoma, who negotiated the alliance between the previously bitter rivals Satsuma and Chōshū. While the Yamauchi clan supported kōbu gattai, (cooperation between the emperor and shōgun), the domain would side with the anti-shōgunate faction when the Boshin War broke out. This was in part due to their most elite corps, the jinshōtai, who fought at Toba-Fushimi on their own volition; while Tosa leadership initially wanted to punish them, they were pardoned when imperial banners appeared on the field. The jinshōtai wore distinctive red "bear wigs" known as shaguma, which were typically reserved for officers (black in the case of Satsuma and Tottori, and white for Chōshū). They had a strict code of conduct and honour: pillaging and harming civilians was forbidden, and mercy was shown to surrendering enemies as they were respected as fair combatants doing their duty. Offending members not just in the jinshōtai but Tosa's army as a whole were executed for any transgressions, being branded as criminals for "bringing shame" to the imperial cause. As Tosa's finest, the jinshōtai participated in many battles throughout the war, including a showdown against the shinsengumi at Kōshū-Katsunuma and the particularly brutal fighting in Aizu.
{Japan_Itagaki_Taisuke_descr_short}Itagaki Taisuke, samurai of Tosa (Revolver, Smith & Wesson No.2 and katana)\nISHIN ONLY

{Japan_Kachi}Kachi
{Japan_Kachi_descr}Era: 1860s\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Nagae yari (Pike)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan & Tokugawa shōgunate\n\nThese warriors take to the field in the traditional style, wearing heavy armour and wielding long spears like their ancestors during the Sengoku jidai. While the face of warfare has changed, the spear remains a powerful weapon and still has some merit in battle. It is far more ergonomic and has superior reach to a rifle with a bayonet, and acts as a strong deterrent against enemy cavalry. Indeed, modern soldiers who are accustomed to ranged warfare will find themselves severely outmatched against a body of men armed solely for close combat!\n\n"But those were the days when, in Chōshū, the anti-foreign fever was at its burning point. Throughout the clan, everyone was prepared for war. Not only men, but women also, were armed with spears, with their hair tied up in a white band."\n-Itō Hirobumi, Samurai of Chōshū and Prime Minister of Japan\n\nAlthough Japan had enjoyed centuries of peace under Tokugawa rule, the samurai class was still expected to train for battle. With the lack of wars and a mandate to wear swords in public, swordsmanship would flourish most during the Edo period, as more potent battlefield weapons were impractical for daily life. However, samurai schools would continue to teach students in the use of yari, yumi, and tanegashima. This would prove useful during the bakumatsu ("End of the shōgunate"), with rising tensions once again threatening the stability of the country. Samurai equipped in the traditional style were a common sight during the numerous conflicts of the mid-19th century, such as the Tengutō Rebellion, Chōshū expeditions, and various insurrections in Kyōto. Even as armies modernized in anticipation of open war, soldiers on both sides would still be equipped in archaic fashion during the early stages of the Boshin War.
{Japan_Kachi_descr_short}Kachi (Pike, Nagae yari)

{Japan_Kaga_Hoheitai}Kaga Hoheitai
{Japan_Kaga_Hoheitai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Springfield M1855 (Rifled musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan & Tokugawa shōgunate (Kaga-han)\n\nTranslation: "Kaga Infantry"\n\nThe samurai of Kaga domain serve as the standard line infantry of the Kansai region beginning in 1865. They can be recruited by both the Tokugawa shōgunate and ishin Japan.\n\nKaga domain (also known as Kanazawa) was ruled by the Maeda clan beginning in 1583, with Oda Nobunaga's appointment of Maeda Toshiie, a talented general and perhaps the most famous kabukimono of the Sengoku jidai. The Maeda had split loyalties during the Sekigahara campaign; the clan head Maeda Toshinaga supported the Tokugawa, while his younger brother Maeda Toshimasa sided with the Toyotomi. Though this earned them the title of tozama ("outsider") daimyō, they were treated generously under the Tokugawa shōgunate. Kaga domain was the largest and wealthiest in all of Japan, and was praised by the shōgun as a prime example of effective governance. This wealth was largely used for cultural and civil pursuits rather than militarism, though the Maeda were wise enough to modernize their forces during the bakumatsu period. While officially loyal to the shōgunate, the Maeda sympathized with the sonnō jōi cause; they did fight against Chōshū forces during the Hamaguri incident, yet attempted to mediate on their behalf when the shōgunate sought punitive action. Kaga still supported the Tokugawa at the beginning of the Boshin War, but quickly pledged allegiance to the new imperial government after the Battle of Toba-Fushimi. The Kaga samurai would fight with the imperial army during the battles of Hokuetsu, Aizu, and Yonezawa.
{Japan_Kaga_Hoheitai_descr_short}Kaga Hoheitai (Rifle-Musket, Springfield M1855 and katana)\nKANSAI AOR

{Japan_Karasugumi}Sendai Karasugumi
{Japan_Karasugumi_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Pattern 1861 Enfield (Rifled musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Sendai-han)\n\nTranslation: "Crow brigade"\n\nThe karasugumi are deadly sharpshooters from Sendai domain. Considered a modern successor to the shinobi of old, they are experts at guerrilla warfare. Accurate marksmen and skilled swordsmen, these men can hide in any terrain, making them are a powerful asset for a resourceful, and perhaps somewhat underhanded general!\n\n"Therefore it is my intention to join the [Northern] Alliance, raise great justice and end chaos, and sustain the imperial land. Not only great and small retainers, but the common people as well, should make determination their object and assist me, let there not be any forgetfulness on this."\n-Date Yoshikuni, Daimyō of Sendai\n\nSendai's sudden (and unwanted) entry into the Boshin War saw an urgent need to rapidly militarize, largely with British assistance. Part of these efforts saw the formation of the karasugumi. They were an irregular corps composed mostly of commoners, as their captain, Hosoya Jūdayū, viewed samurai as weak-willed and unreliable. Feared for their all-black attire and expertise in ambush tactics, they were said to be the revival of Date Masamune's shinobi corps! Members of the karasugumi would convince the gakuheitai to regroup with shogunate loyalists on Ezo after Sendai's surrender, where they would take part in the Battle of Hakodate.
