{Japan_Kenyotai}Ōgaki Ken'yōtai
{Japan_Kenyotai_descr}Era: 1860s\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Tachi (Sword)\n\nTranslation: "Hybrid Corps"\n\nThe ken'yōtai of Ōgaki domain can be recruited by both the Tokugawa shōgunate and ishin Japan in the Kansai region. They are pure shock troops who fight with swords alone. Though they will find themselves outmatched in melee by armoured warriors, their light equipment allows them to move faster and potentially close the distance with ranged infantry while taking fewer volleys—and most riflemen are far less of a match.\n\nŌgaki domain was granted to the Toda clan as a reward for Toda Ujikane's service during the Shimabara Rebellion, a Catholic revolt in 1637–1638 and a contributing factor for Japan's policy of isolation throughout the Edo period. During the bakumatsu, Ōgaki was one of only a few domains outside of western Japan that attempted to reform its army during the 1850s, though these efforts were largely unsuccessful at the time. This included the creation of a specialized corps recruited from the domain's swordsmanship schools called the ken'yōtai, who wore tall conical helmets rather than the round manjūgasa of their regular troops. They fought for the shōgunate during the Hamaguri Rebellion (1864) as well as the Second Chōshū Expedition (1866). With the outbreak of the Boshin War came their most significant combat action. During the Battle of Toba-Fushimi, the armies of Ōgaki and Aizu engaged in fierce fighting against Satsuma forces, but ultimately retreated when the imperial banners appeared on the field. The daimyō of Ōgaki, Toda Ujitaka, appealed to the imperial court for forgiveness, and a pardon would surprisingly be given. As a result, Ōgaki was effectively able to "defect" to the imperial cause, and would fight alongside the Satchō forces for the remainder of the war.
{Japan_Kenyotai_descr_short}Ōgaki Ken'yōtai (Sword, Tachi)\nKANSAI AOR

{Japan_Kiheitai}Chōshū Kiheitai
{Japan_Kiheitai_descr}Era: 1860s (1863)\nQuality: Semi-professional\nPrimary: Springfield M1863 (Rifled musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan (Chōshū-han)\n\nTranslation: "Irregular force"\n\nThe kiheitai are light infantry available to ishin Japan, and their first unit to be armed with rifles. As commoners they are cheaper and have higher replenishment than samurai, without compromising morale or discipline. They are capable in melee as well, though they will find themselves outmatched by the samurai from domains most renowned for their martial prowess.\n\n"Bold and fierce are the [kiheitai] soldiers—and what is their intention? Each one offers himself, repaying the nation with his death. It should be our fate to join the ranks of the mourned; how shameful then, to be among the mourners..."\n -Takasugi Shinsaku, Samurai of Chōshū, Commander of the kiheitai\n\nAs part of the fixed social hierarchy of the Edo period, military service was effectively restricted to samurai. During the bakumatsu, however, there was a renewed interest in reintroducing commoners into the army. One of the earliest examples could be seen in Chōshū domain. A samurai named Takasugi Shinsaku had traveled to Shanghai and witnessed firsthand European military superiority and their exploitation of China. Fearing that the same could happen to Japan, he formed a fully western-style force: the kiheitai, made up of volunteers from all social classes. They saw their first combat action in 1863 during the Shimonoseki campaign, and although Chōshū's largely traditional army stood no chance against the British, the kiheitai were praised for their discipline; it is said that they held their ground even as their allies fled around them. This effectively proved the worth of Takasugi's methods, and by the time the shōgunate launched a punitive expedition in 1866, Chōshū's standing army was modernized using the kiheitai as a model. The kiheitai were battle-hardened veterans even before the Boshin War broke out, and proved themselves as highly effective troops despite many being of humble birth. They fought in nearly every major battle, including Toba-Fushimi, Hokuetsu, Aizu, and Hakodate.
{Japan_Kiheitai_descr_short}Chōshū Kiheitai (Rifle-Musket, Springfield M1863 and katana)\nCHŪGOKU AOR\nISHIN ONLY

{Japan_Kijutai}Shōnai Kijūtai
{Japan_Kijutai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Elite\nPrimary: Whitworth Rifle Model 1857 w/ Davidson scope (Rifled musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Shōnai-han)\n\nTranslation: "Special rifle force"\n\nThese expert sharpshooters from Shōnai domain can be recruited by the Tokugawa shōgunate in Tōhoku, beginning in 1865. Using scoped Whitworth rifles, they have exceptional range and accuracy at the cost of longer reload times than a breech-loader. As samurai of Shōnai, they also possess very strong morale and are happy to draw swords should the need arise. Leaving them on the receiving end of enemy fire or a cavalry charge will be a waste of their talents, however.\n\nThe Sakai were one of the most prestigious clans under the Tokugawa shōgunate; Sakai Tadatsugu was one of the legendary "Four Heavenly Kings" of Tokugawa Ieyasu. During the bakumatsu, they ruled over numerous powerful domains, including Himeji, Obama, and Shōnai. With the outbreak of the Boshin War, the Sakai clan sided with the shōgunate loyalists; the armies of Himeji and Obama fought at Toba-Fushimi, while Shōnai troops attacked the Satsuma residence in Edo. The Satchō Alliance was particularly concerned after Shōnai joined the Northern Alliance, as it was a very wealthy domain that had rapidly modernized with the assistance of Prussian arms dealers. Their equipment rivalled even the most powerful western domains, including Peabody, Sharps, and Spencer rifles. Some Shōnai soldiers were even armed with the rare Whitworth sniper rifles. The Shōnai army fought with distinction during the Battle of Hokuetsu, but their most successful actions took place after Akita defected from the Northern Alliance. Shōnai and Morioka dispatched an invasion force and easily crushed the Akita forces, who were still largely armed in medieval fashion, but would gradually be pushed back as Satchō reinforcements arrived. In addition to their superior firepower, Shōnai soldiers were noted to be especially brave; on numerous occasions, Shōnai units fought to the last man, launching suicidal charges after running out of bullets. Shōnai was the last domain to surrender during the Boshin War; a fact that the people of this region take pride in to this day.

{Japan_Matsushiro_Hoheitai}Matsushiro Hoheitai
{Japan_Matsushiro_Hoheitai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Springfield M1861 (Rifled musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan & Tokugawa shōgunate (Matsushiro-han)\n\nTranslation: "Matsushiro Infantry"\n\nThe Sanada clan of Matsushiro domain provide the standard line infantry for the Kantō region beginning in 1865. They can be recruited by either the Tokugawa shōgunate or ishin Japan.\n\n"Eastern ethics, Western science."\n-Sakuma Shōzan, Samurai of Matsushiro\n\nThough the Sanada clan are most famous for Sanada Yukimura's valiant attempt on Tokugawa Ieyasu's life during the Siege of Osaka, his brother Nobuyuki had actually fought for the other side. This was part of a plan to ensure their clan's survival regardless of the war's outcome, and as a result, the Sanada were treated generously despite being a tozama ("outsider") clan. During the bakumatsu, they ruled over Matsushiro domain and were responsible for security at Edo Bay. Domain retainer Sakuma Shōzan emerged as one of the most renowned rangaku ("western learning") scholars in Japan, tutoring many famous samurai including Katsu Kaishū, Saigō Takamori, Sakamoto Ryoma, and Yamamoto Kakuma. He sought to strengthen his country through a philosophy combining Japanese spirit with western technology, and was a key leader of the kōbu gattai movement. Though he would be assassinated by sōnno jōi revolutionaries in 1864, he had already helped set many changes into motion. After the outbreak of the Boshin War, Matsushiro was the first and most prominent domain in Shinano to side with the new imperial army. As part of Shōzan's reforms, they were able to effectively modernize their army and were the only domain in Japan to possess Parrott guns. They fought with distinction in the brutal back-and-forth struggle for Nagaoka Castle during the Battle of Hokuetsu, and are perhaps best known for using their artillery to devastating effect during the Battle of Aizu.
{Japan_Matsushiro_Hoheitai_descr_short}Matsushiro Hoheitai (Rifle-Musket, Springfield M1861 and katana)\nKANTŌ AOR

{Japan_Mori_Takachika}Mōri Takachika
{Japan_Mori_Takachika_descr}Mōri Takachika, daimyō of Chōshū.
{Japan_Mori_Takachika_descr_short}Mōri Takachika, daimyō of Chōshū (Revolver, Smith & Wesson No.2 and katana)\nISHIN ONLY

{Japan_Nagaoka_Hoheitai}Nagaoka Hoheitai
{Japan_Nagaoka_Hoheitai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Springfield M1865 (Breech-loading rifle)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Nagaoka-han)\n\nTranslation: "Infantry force"\n\nAvailable from 1865, Nagaoka's hoheitai are a highly professional line infantry force, well-armed with breech-loading Tabatière Mle.1853/67 rifles. Unusually for line infantry, they are experts at hiding in woods and have a high charge bonus, making them particularly dangerous ambushers if the terrain allows.\n\n"How could I turn against the favour given by the 300 years of the Tokugawa shogunate? We are at the battlefield by the order of Nagaoka domain, which has not ordered us to surrender."\n-Yamamoto Tatewaki, Commander of the Nagaoka forces\n\nWhile the Makino clan of Nagaoka domain supported the shōgunate and had troops stationed in Osaka Castle during the Battle of Toba-Fushimi, they did not partake in the fighting and returned home soon afterwards. Chief retainer Kawai Tsugunosuke, inspired by the US Monroe Doctrine, pursued a policy of armed neutrality: though the domain had heavily militarized to deter being threatened or ordered around, they did not intend to join the Boshin War on either side. Upon refusing demands from the new government to attack Aizu, however, Nagaoka would be invaded by the Satchō forces. This caused them to join the Ōuetsu Reppan Dōmei ("Alliance of Northern Domains"). Of the alliance members, Nagaoka had by far the most modern army. Thanks to Prussian arms dealers Henry and Edward Schnell, they possessed thousands of breech-loading rifles and even a pair of Gatling guns (out of 3 in Japan total). This advanced weaponry, along with their familiarity with the forested and mountainous terrain, was used to devastating effect. Though overwhelming in number, the Satchō army suffered heavy casualties during the Battle of Hokuetsu before finally emerging victorious. With Nagaoka in ruins, many resentful soldiers refused to surrender and continued to engage in guerrilla warfare. At least 1,500 troops and civilian refugees retreated to Aizu, around 600 of which would fight in the Battle of Aizu.
{Japan_Nagaoka_Hoheitai_descr_short}Nagaoka Hoheitai (Rifle, Springfield M1865 and katana)\nTŌHOKU AOR\nSHŌGUNATE ONLY

{Japan_Raijintai}Kuwana Raijintai
{Japan_Raijintai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Lorenz M1854 (Rifled musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Kuwana-han)\n\nTranslation: "Thunder God's corps"\n\nThe raijintai are elite sharpshooters, and the most elite unit born from Kuwana's extensive military reforms. Armed with a breech-loading carbine, these men are capable of rapid and accurate fire. They excel in rough and forested terrain, with enhanced stealth and respectable melee stats.\n\n"Tatsumi is a genius of a soldier. Had he been born in France around the time of Napoleon, he probably would have become a general before he turned 30."\n-French military instructor at the Shoheiko academy\n\nAfter the Battle of Sekigahara, Kuwana domain was gifted to one of Ieyasu's greatest generals, Honda Tadakatsu. It was later transferred to the Okudaira-Matsudaira, then to the Hisamatsu-Matsudaira in the 19th century. During the bakumatsu period, the daimyō of Kuwana, Matsudaira Sadaaki, served as Kyōto shoshidai ("Shōgun's Deputy of Kyōto") in support of his brother, Matsudaira Katamori of Aizu. This made him a target of Satchō officials in the new government when the Boshin War broke out, and they demanded the Matsudaira be stripped of their titles and lands. Kuwana's army made up a significant portion of the shōgunate forces at Toba-Fushimi, and the lessons learned from this defeat prompted a reorganization of their military. The raijintai were perhaps the most elite of these new units. Led by Tatsumi Naofumi, regarded as a prodigy in military strategy by his French instructors, they were armed with modern rifles and fought using guerrilla tactics. Their most notable victory was during the Battle of Hokuetsu, where they took the head of kiheitai captain, Tokiyama Naohachi. They later took part in the defence of Aizu, until Katamori asked his brother to request aid from other northern domains. Sadaaki reluctantly left Aizu, but was unable to muster any reinforcements. The forces under Naofumi surrendered to the new government in Shōnai shortly after the collapse of the Northern Alliance, while Sadaaki himself joined the Republic of Ezo and took part in the Battle of Hakodate.
{Japan_Raijintai_descr_short}Kuwana Raijintai (Rifle-Musket, Lorenz M1854 and katana)\nKANSAI AOR\nSHŌGUNATE ONLY

{Japan_Revolver_Kihei}Sampeitai Kihei
{Japan_Revolver_Kihei_descr}Era: 1860s\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Smith & Wesson No.2 (Revolver)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Bakufu)\n\nTranslation: "Three-branch corps cavalry"\n\nThese light horsemen are the first modern cavalry of the Tokugawa shōgunate. They are highly mobile and can fire from a distance, though are still greatly outranged by rifle-armed infantry. Japanese cavalry are generally subpar compared to those from nations with stronger equestrian traditions, such as the Qing.\n\n"Though my pistol held six bullets, since I’d only loaded five I only had one shot left...Seeing [the assassin], I cocked my pistol again. Miyoshi [Shinzō] was standing there with his spear; I used his left shoulder as a gun mount—and taking aim at the man’s chest, I fired. It looked as though I’d hit him. He lay on his belly crawling forward, as if about to die."\n-Sakamoto Ryōma, Samurai of Tosa\n\nAs Japanese armies modernized during the bakumatsu period, mounted samurai began to shift from the traditional heavy lancer to western-style light cavalry with firearms. The Japanese were familiar with handguns before the reopening of the country, having developed the bajō-zutsu matchlock and importing Dutch-style flintlocks, but soon realized the advantages of the revolver. While the French Lefaucheux was the most commonly imported from the 1850s to early 1860s, it was overtaken by American revolvers after the end of their civil war. The most ubiquitous was Smith & Wesson's No.2. It was famously carried by Sakamoto Ryōma, who with the help of his bodyguard was able to fend off 10 spear-armed assassins in what is known as the Teradaya incident. With its widespread use in the Boshin War, the Japanese quickly gained an affinity for the Smith & Wesson design. The No.3 model was officially adopted by the Imperial Japanese Army and Navy, with large numbers purchased between 1878 and 1908 (more than any country aside from Russia). It heavily influenced the design of Japan's first modern domestic handgun, the Type 26.
{Japan_Revolver_Kihei_descr_short}Sampeitai Kihei (Revolver, Smith & Wesson No.2 and katana)\nSHŌGUNATE ONLY

{Japan_Rikuguntai_1900}Teikoku Rikuguntai (1900s)
{Japan_Rikuguntai_1900_descr}Era: 1900s\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Type 30 Arisaka (Repeating rifle)\nSecondary: Bayonet\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan (Imperial Japanese Army)\n\nTranslation: "Army force"\n\nThe Russo-Japanese War may have marked the first time an Asian nation defeated a western power, but it was at a great cost. This baptism by fire highlighted the shortcomings in the Imperial Japanese Army's doctrine and equipment, notably the conspicuous uniforms and unreliability of the Type 30 Arisaka in trench warfare. Ever adaptable, the Japanese have rearmed and retrained their forces to be better suited for the new face of warfare, putting these lessons so brutally learned into practice.\n\n"After weeks in rain-filled trenches, the [Japanese] force was only too eager to begin the hand-to-hand encounter...[They] started their charge up the steep slopes of the Tsingtau mountainous fortresses that rose one thousand feet high."\n-Jefferson Jones, American war journalist\n\nJapan was one of the first countries to switch to a camouflaged uniform, thanks to their early experience of modern warfare in the Russo-Japanese War. It quickly became apparent that in a war with machine guns and repeating rifles on both sides, the navy blue uniforms worn since the formation of the Imperial Japanese Army made soldiers easy targets for enemy fire. Khaki uniforms were adopted shortly into the conflict, though it would not be until 1906 when they were issued to all troops. As an ally of Britain, and eager to expand their colonial empire, Japan entered World War I on the side of the Entente and swiftly mobilized to capture German possessions in China and the Pacific. The largest battle in this theatre occurred on the outskirts of Tsingtao, where the Japanese were faced with formidable fortresses and trench networks on the hilltops overlooking the port city—not unlike their engagements against Russia a decade earlier. The Imperial Japanese Navy pioneered the use of aircraft to bombard both naval and ground targets, and at one point the sole German pilot engaged and shoot down a Japanese aviator with his pistol, marking the first dogfight and aerial kill in history.

{Japan_Saga_Hoheitai}Saga Yūheitai
{Japan_Saga_Hoheitai_descr}Era: 1860s (1866)\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Remington Rolling Block (Breech-loading rifle)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan (Saga-han)\n\nTranslation: "Irregular force"\n\nSaga domain contributes capable light infantry to fight for the Satchō Alliance. They are capable of a high rate of reasonably accurate fire thanks to their breech-loading rifles, but are not especially strong swordsmen compared to the samurai of other domains, particularly Aizu or Satsuma.\n\nThe Nabeshima clan of Saga domain were in a uniquely privileged position during the Edo period, thanks to their proximity to Nagasaki, the only city that permitted foreign trade after the implementation of sakoku. While the trading ports themselves were directly overseen by shōgunate officials, Saga was responsible for its security and as a result was more exposed to western influences. In particular, Saga established the first modern artillery foundry in Japan, and produced Armstrong guns for their army. Saga was a relatively late member of the Satchō Alliance, first seeing combat in the Battle of Ueno where their Armstrong guns were the deciding factor in their victory. They also played a pivotal role in the Akita War, when Akita domain defected from the Northern Alliance to pledge allegiance to the new government. Morioka and Shōnai invaded Akita in retaliation, and their highly modernized forces easily crushed the Akita army, which was still armed in medieval fashion with spears and bows. Reinforcements were swiftly dispatched, with Saga samurai making up the bulk of the relief force. One of their main units were the yūheitai, comprised of around 800 men. They were exceptionally well-armed; nearly half were equipped with Spencer repeating carbines, and the rest had various breech-loading rifles. Their timely arrival quickly turned the tide of the battle in favour of the new government, preventing the fall of Akita and forcing the Morioka and Shōnai forces to retreat. To this day, the people of Akita and Saga share a strong bond, despite being from opposite sides of Japan.

{Japan_Sampeitai}Sampeitai
{Japan_Sampeitai_descr}Era: 1860s\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Fusil d'infanterie modèle 1857 (Rifled musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Bakufu)\n\nTranslation: "Three-branch corps"\n\nThe sampeitai are the first modern infantry available to the Tokugawa shōgunate. They are a fully professional force with good discipline and morale. Though reasonably trained, they are still armed with muzzle-loading rifles, meaning good positioning or a well-timed charge may be needed to take on better-equipped foes. They are still samurai after all, with a touch of French élan!\n\nThe modernization efforts of the Tokugawa saw the creation of a centralized professional army. Formed in 1862 by the graduates of several samurai academies, the sampeitai were drawn from the shōgun's personal lands as well as those of various daimyō. Their name translates to "three-branch corps," consisting of infantry, cavalry, and artillery. They initially wore traditional Japanese attire, but as France was considered to have the strongest army in Europe at the time and hoped to become allies with Japan, French-style doctrine and uniforms would be adopted during the reign of Tokugawa Yoshinobu. A French military mission was dispatched to Japan in 1867 along with modern artillery and breech-loading rifles, though the majority of the sampeitai would still be armed with muzzle-loaders during the outbreak of the Boshin War. Regardless, they fought valiantly throughout the conflict, from the initial clash at Toba-Fushimi to the final stand at Hakodate.
{Japan_Sampeitai_descr_short}Sampeitai (Rifle-Musket, Fusil d'infanterie modèle 1857 and katana)\nSHŌGUNATE ONLY

{Japan_Satsuma_Hoheitai}Satsuma Shōjūtai
{Japan_Satsuma_Hoheitai_descr}Era: 1860s (1866)\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Snider-Enfield Mk.I (Breech-loading rifle)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan (Satsuma-han)\n\nTranslation: "Rifle corps"\n\nSatsuma's shojutai will become available to the ishin faction following the creation of the Satchō Alliance. Easily distinguished by their tall jingasa helmets and armed with breech-loading P1860 Wilson-Enfields, they are a valuable component of any army and can be relied on in most situations.\n\nWhile the official stance of the domain in the early 1860s was that of kōbu gattai (unity between the shōgun and imperial court), the presence of many sonnō jōi ("Expel the barbarians, revere the emperor") partisans in Satsuma led to an incident on the road during a trip to discuss reforms in Edo. This resulted in the assassination of a British merchant in 1862, and violent retaliation would soon follow. After being on the receiving end of British firepower during the bombardment of Kagoshima in 1863, a peace treaty would be signed that led to a close friendship between Satsuma and Britain. The domain began to rapidly militarize with their assistance, and even purchased British steamships for its navy. When the shōgunate launched a punitive Chōshū Expedition in 1864, Satsuma retainer Saigō Takamori helped negotiate a peaceful settlement; despite a bitter rivalry between the two domains, there were fears that if Chōshū were neutralized, Satsuma would be next. An alliance between the two domains was established through the mediation of the Tosa samurai, Sakamoto Ryoma, in 1866.
{Japan_Satsuma_Hoheitai_descr_short}Satsuma Shōjūtai (Rifle, Snider-Enfield Mk.I and katana)\nKYŪSHŪ AOR\nISHIN ONLY

{Japan_Satsuma_Tojotai}Satsuma Tojōtai
{Japan_Satsuma_Tojotai_descr}Era: 1860s (1866)\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Sharps M1863 (Breech-loading carbine)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan (Satsuma-han)\n\nTranslation: "Outside castle force"\n\nThese skilled sharpshooters hail from Satsuma domain and are well-armed with breech-loading carbines. They are capable of a high rate of fire, though are somewhat limited in range and accuracy as they still use black powder weaponry. Although the samurai of Satsuma are renowned for their skill, these troops are fewer in number and lack the stomach for melee of their counterparts in the line infantry.\n\n"While exposed to this galling fire, and unable to deploy his men, [Ōkubo Toshimichi] gave the order to fall back to a better position, when he found himself suddenly attacked in the rear...But for his two companies of sharpshooters in front, who made excellent practice in picking off the enemy’s gunners, his command would wellnigh have been annihilated."\n-William H. Seward, U.S. Secretary of State\n\nAs part of their modernization efforts, Satsuma developed a sharpshooter corps sometime after the bombardment of Kagoshima in 1863. Recruitment was still limited to samurai, though military doctrine and organization now closely followed western models. While the majority of Satsuma's soldiers were armed with British rifles, large quantities of American Civil War surplus were purchased as well, particularly a wide variety of breech-loading cavalry carbines. Satsuma's sharpshooters played a major role during the Battle of Toba-Fushimi, where they fired the first shots of the battle (and thus the Boshin War). In particular, the two companies under the command of Ōkubo Toshimichi are credited with covering the retreat of his men when their position was outflanked by shōgunate forces. The most famous of Satsuma's sharpshooters was Murata Tsuneyoshi, renowned as the finest marksman in the army. He led the Tojō ichibantai ("First Outer Castle Unit") in the battles of Toba-Fushimi and Bonari Pass, and the siege of Aizuwakamatsu castle. Murata was promoted to Captain of the Imperial Guard in 1871, and in 1875 he was sent to Europe to study firearms development. Although prohibited from visiting military facilities due to fears of an eastern nation becoming too powerful, he was able to acquire a Gras and Beaumont. He used these as inspiration for his own design, adopted in 1880 as the Type 13 Murata: Japan's first domestically produced service rifle.
{Japan_Satsuma_Tojotai_descr_short}Satsuma Tojōtai (Rifle-Musket, Pattern 1858 Enfield and katana)\nKYŪSHŪ AOR\nISHIN ONLY

{Japan_Seishi}Seishi
{Japan_Seishi_descr}Era: 1860s\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Yari (Lance)\nSecondary: Tachi (Sword)\n\nSeishi are the first cavalry unit recruitable by Japan. They fight in the traditional style, wearing heavy armour and wielding long lances, culminating in a devastating charge. Such equipment comes at a higher cost, however, and their reduced mobility can make all the difference when attempting to close in before a volley.\n\n"I would not use a weapon I am not used to, even if it meant dying."\n-Katō Isshaku, Samurai of Nagaoka\n\nThough samurai were expected to be versed in a variety of fighting methods, they originally fought as mounted archers; their armour was specifically designed to balance protection with full range of motion. During the Sengoku jidai, the Takeda clan pioneered the use of shock cavalry, and from then on the spear became the weapon of choice for mounted samurai. Even after the introduction of matchlock firearms, including the development of native Japanese pistol designs, samurai cavalry would maintain their preference for close combat throughout the Edo period.
{Japan_Seishi_descr_short}Seishi (Lance, Yari and tachi)

{Japan_Shinsenryodan}Shinsenryodan
{Japan_Shinsenryodan_descr}Era: 1870s\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Dreyse Zündnadelgewehr M-1862 (Breech-loading rifle)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan (Metropolitan Police)\n\nTranslation: "??"\n\nText\n\n"Many of the soldiers of the shinsenryodan were samurai from the various feudal domains of the [northern] Tōhoku region, making it difficult to control them. However, on the battlefield, they were truly brave and elite. Even the Satsuma soldiers sang, 'If it were not for the imperial guard artillery and volunteer policemen, we would have rushed into the beautiful city of Edo.'"\n-Yoshio Matsushita, Lieutenant of the Imperial Japanese Army\n\nWhen the Satsuma Rebellion broke out in 1877, the Imperial Japanese Army was still in its infancy, lacking in manpower and combat experience. Large numbers of now former samurai volunteered, though imperial officials worried that enlisting them into the army would essentially be admitting that conscription had failed. Instead, they were recruited into the Metropolitan Police. Many were veterans of the Boshin War, and those from the domains that were defeated were especially eager to take revenge on the samurai of Satsuma. One of the most elite police units was the shinsenryodan, formed by Tatsumi Naofumi, who commanded Kuwana's raijintai during the Boshin War. This force was recruited from throughout the Tōhoku region (the north of mainland Japan) including Aizu, Kuwana, Sendai, and Yonezawa. The shinsenryodan excelled in combat, and it was rumoured among the Satsuma rebels that former shinsengumi were within their ranks, further adding to their fearsome reputation. They took part in the fierce fighting in Nobeoka and were among the imperial forces during the final battle at Shiroyama.
{Japan_Shinsenryodan_descr_short}Shinsenryodan (Rifle, Dreyse Zündnadelgewehr M-62 and katana)\nISHIN ONLY

{Japan_Shizoku}Shizoku
{Japan_Shizoku_descr}Era: 1870s\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Snider-Enfield Mk.I (Breech-loading rifle)\nSecondary: Katana (sword)\nAllegiance: Locals / Mercenaries\n\nTranslation: "Warrior families"\n\nTest\n\nAlthough the Boshin War had ended in the restoration of imperial rule, it was not the end of internal strife in Japan. The new government passed many controversial reforms in their efforts to westernize the nation. In 1873, the samurai lost their traditional role as warriors with the creation of a conscript-based imperial army, and their hereditary stipends and privileges were revoked. Most significant, however, was the haitōrei ("Sword abolishment act") in 1876, which made it illegal to wear swords in public. This enraged many of the former samurai, or shizoku ("warrior families"), who had helped bring down the Tokugawa shōgunate; those who resonated with the order to "expel the barbarians" believed the government they fought for had betrayed them, while others understood that changes were necessary, but protested that they were made too quickly without proper compensation for their service or time to adapt to the new way of life forced upon them. Revolts would break out in the domains that had brought the emperor to power, starting with Saga in 1874 and followed by Kumamoto, Akizuki, and Chōshū in 1876. The largest would occur in Satsuma in 1877, led by Saigō Takamori. Although one of the most important supporters of the imperial cause, he resigned from his post and established private military schools to train disaffected shizoku from across Japan into professional soldiers. As Satsuma still possessed stockpiles of modern rifles and artillery used in the Boshin War, and the Kagoshima Arsenal was the main supplier of Snider-Enfield rifles and ammunition for the Imperial Japanese Army, the rebels were well-equipped and proved to be a formidable foe for the new government's forces.
{Japan_Shizoku_descr_short}Shizoku (Rifle, Snider-Enfield Mk.I and katana)

{Japan_Shotai}Chōshū Shotai

{Japan_Suzakutai}Aizu Suzakutai
{Japan_Suzakutai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Bavarian Gewehr M-1842/51 (Rifled musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Aizu-han)\n\nTranslation: "Vermilion Bird Force"\n\nThe suzakutai are a more premium line infantry unit available to the Tokugawa shogunate beginning in 1865, and are recruited from the Tōhoku region. With rifled muskets, they are a step up from the teppōtai at a more premium cost. Where these troops truly stand out is through their strong morale and swordsmanship, showing the indomitable spirit of the Aizu samurai!\n\n"No matter how many tens of thousands of enemies there are, our elite troops of Aizu will annihilate them all at once."\n-Matsudaira Katamori, Daimyō of Aizu\n\nAizu was among the most important supporters of the shogunate. Aizu boasted strong martial traditions and were tasked by the shōgunate on to provide security in Ezo as well as Kyōto. Though undoubtedly an honour and a testament to their reputation, these missions were a significant burden on the domain's finances, and while attempts were made to modernize their army it was not until after the outbreak of the Boshin War that full-scale reforms would take place. Aizu reorganized their samurai into age-based units named after the Four Constellations: the byakkotai (White Tigers) aged 16 and 17, the suzakutai (Vermilion Birds) aged 18 to 35, the seiryūtai (Azure Dragons) aged 36 to 49, and the genbutai (Black Tortoises) aged 50 and above. While forces from Aizu were present at Toba-Fushimi, once it was apparent that the Satchō alliance had imperial support, the ruling daimyō Matsudaira Katamori repeatedly apologized and sought reconciliation with the new government. These pleas were rejected by officials from Satsuma and Chōshū, who held a grudge against his service to the Tokugawa family. Katamori was declared an enemy of the court, culminating in the Battle of Aizu: the bloodiest battle of the Boshin War.
{Japan_Suzakutai_descr_short}Aizu Suzakutai (Rifle-Musket, Bavarian Gewehr M-1842/51 and katana)\nTŌHOKU AOR\nSHŌGUNATE ONLY

{Japan_Suzakutai_1870}Aizu Suzakutai (1870s)
{Japan_Suzakutai_1870_descr}Era: 1870s\nQuality: Professional\nPrimary: Dreyse Zündnadelgewehr M-1862 (Breech-loading rifle)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Tokugawa shōgunate (Aizu-han)\n\nTranslation: "Vermilion Bird Force"\n\n"Samurai of Satsuma, see with your own bodies: are the swords of Aizu sharp or dull?"\n-Yamakawa Hiroshi (Ōkura), Samurai of Aizu, Major General of the Imperial Japanese Army\n\nThe Yamamoto clan of Aizu claim descent from Yamamoto Kansuke, one of the legendary Twenty-Four Generals of Takeda Shingen, and served as gunnery instructors for the domain's samurai. As Aizu was one of the principal domains entrusted by the shōgun to defend Japan's coasts, Yamamoto Kakuma was tasked with studying western science under the renowned scholar Sakuma Shōzan. He spearheaded the modernization of Aizu's military, though these efforts would be hampered by the domain's poor finances, worsened after Aizu was also tasked with maintaining peace in Kyōto. This would change after the Hamaguri Gate rebellion in 1864, however, where Kakuma was wounded while commanding Aizu's artillery corps. In 1866, he travelled to Nagasaki where he befriended a Prussian arms dealer, Karl Lehmann, and placed an order for 4,300 Dreyse needle rifles. Unfortunately, the weapons were not delivered in time for the Boshin War. Kakuma was unable to participate due to going blind from the injuries he sustained during the Hamaguri Gate rebellion, and spent the war in Satsuma captivity. His younger brother Saburo would be killed in the Battle of Toba-Fushimi, and his father Gonpachi in the Battle of Aizu. His sister, Yae, is perhaps the most famous of the onna-bushi of Aizu, fighting with a Spencer rifle that Kakuma was gifted by Lehmann. The Dreyse rifles ordered by Kakuma were later issued to units of the Metropolitan Police during the Satsuma Rebellion.

{Japan_Teppotai}Teppōtai
{Japan_Teppotai_descr}Era: 1860s\nQuality: Semi-professional\nPrimary: Imported "geberu" (Smoothbore musket)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan & Tokugawa shōgunate\n\nTranslation: "Gun corps"\n\nThe teppōtai are the basic garrison unit of Japan in the early period. While their name is a holdover from earlier periods and implies the use of older-style Tanegashima teppō, they are armed with a variety of imported muskets (primary Dutch and German) known as "geberu". Enlistment was still restricted to samurai, and they have more resilient morale and a better appetite for melee than the militias of most nations. However, this results in a smaller population being eligible for recruitment, and does little to make up for their outdated equipment.\n\nJapanese modernization efforts predated the forced reopening of the country in 1853. Despite the official policy of isolation, the country was still exposed to foreign ideas and technology through Dutch traders in Nagasaki, known as rangaku ("Dutch studies"). An official of Nagasaki, Takashima Shūhan, noticed the military gap and began to study western weapons and tactics. He purchased Dutch firearms and set up an artillery foundry for his military academy in 1834; many of his recruits came from Satsuma, Saga, and Chōshū, as they were wary of the western ships that had been spotted from their shores. China's defeat in the First Opium War in 1840 alarmed officials within the Tokugawa shōgunate, and Shūhan argued that modernization was necessary for Japan's survival. He demonstrated his Dutch-styled infantry and artillery corps to the following year with promising results. In 1843, shōgun Tokugawa Ieyoshi authourized the procurement of western firearms and cannons, and promoted the study of European tactics for national security. Conservative elements within the administration would remain, however, preventing rapid militarization until Matthew Perry's expedition.
{Japan_Teppotai_descr_short}Teppōtai (Musket, Imported "geberu" and katana)

{Japan_Yamagunitai}Yamagunitai
{Japan_Yamagunitai_descr}Era: 1860s (1865)\nQuality: Elite\nPrimary: Albini-Braendlin M1867 (Breech-loading rifle)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan (Imperial Court / Tottori-han)\n\nTranslation: "Yamaguni corps"\n\nUpon successfully obtaining imperial favour, ishin Japan can recruit the yamagunitai in Kyōto. Trusted with carrying the imperial banner into battle, they are the predecessors of the imperial guard and the finest line infantry available to this faction in the early period.\n\n"This is an order to vanquish the emperor's enemy; Don't you see the imperial banner?; Go all the way, go all the way!"\n-Shinagawa Yajirō, "Miya-san, Miya-san"\n\nThe Yamaguni hamlet near Kyoto has strong ties to the imperial family, being a personal estate of the emperor and playing a major role in the construction of Heian-kyo (ancient Kyoto). The people of this region took great pride in their history, and beginning in 1867 a volunteer force of peasants began training as guards of Kyoto. After the Battle of Toba-Fushimi, imperial official Iwakura Tomomi formally organized them into the yamagunitai, made up of two companies: one would continue guarding the emperor in Kyoto, while the other was attached to the army of Tottori domain. This earned them the nickname shimpeigumi ("Company of Imperial Guards"), and alongside the Tottori forces they would fight in the battles of Kōshū-Katsunuma, Yasuzaka, and Ueno. With the Japanese mainland secure, they returned to Kyoto where they were entrusted with guarding the imperial brocade in the victory procession.

{Japan_Yumitai}Yumitai
{Japan_Yumitai_descr}Era: 1860s\nQuality: Semi-professional\nPrimary: Daikyū (Bow)\nSecondary: Katana (Sword)\nAllegiance: Ishin Japan & Tokugawa shōgunate\n\nYumitai are a basic ranged unit recruitable from the start of the campaign. While lacking the killing potential of guns, they don't require direct line of sight to shoot, making them potentially useful in sieges and rougher terrain. Like all samurai they are capable in melee, particularly thanks to thei traditional armour.\n\nAlthough guns had surpassed bows as the primary ranged weapon in Japanese warfare since the late 16th century, archers still had the advantage of being able to shoot over friendly troops and could provide covering fire while arquebusiers reloaded. During the peaceful Edo period, many samurai continued to practice archery (kyūjutsu) as an art form, and prestigious competitions were regularly held where practitioners across Japan could demonstrate their skill. It remained a part of military education in many domains as well, though soldiers would carry guns and spears when they mobilized for battle. This would have been the case in the Boshin War, with the bow typically being absent on the battlefield. The most extreme exception was the army of Akita domain, which had not modernized at all and was primarily composed of archers; they were easily crushed by the modernized forces of Shōnai and Morioka, who didn't suffer a single casualty in the opening engagements. The abolition of the samurai class after the war threatened the survival Japan's traditional arts, with martial arts in particular being viewed as an obsolete relic of the past. To keep the practice alive, kyūjutsu masters began to open their own dōjō and encouraged both former samurai and commoners to join. By the 1890s, archery was adopted as a subject in general public education.
{Japan_Yumitai_descr_short}Yumitai (Longbow, Daikyū and katana)

{kaiserjager}Grenzers 
{kaiserjager_descr}Grenzers (Rifle-Musket, Lorenz Model 1854)(German for 'borderers' or 'border troops') were units of soldiers that had existed as part of the Austrian military since the days of Napoleon Bonaparte. By the time of the establishment of the union with Hungary in 1867, they were effectively a sort of mobile Militia unit that specialized in fighting in mountainous terrain. Most of the soldiers were Croatian and most of the officers were Hungarian. When the Austro-Hungarian Empire acquired the Bosnia region in 1878, a number of Grenzer units were deployed there, where they fought against the bandits and dissident groups operating in the mountains.
{kaiserjager_descr_short}Grenzers (Rifle-Musket, Lorenz Model 1854)

{khi_150mm_descr}The 150 MM Guns (15 cm) have a ton of variations, being used by many countries around the world. These guns have a very high range compared to other artillery and fire bigger shells than regular artillery, serving for siege and defensive purposes mainly. The Germans developed the 15cm Kanone 16, used during WW1 and WW2, it would fire 2 types of highly explosive shells and served mainly for coastal defense during WW2. The 15cm L/40 Feldkanone also appeared in WW1, the Germans were desperate for long-range artillery, so they designed this new heavy artillery, it was mainly used in rails and mounted in platforms, however, land versions existed. The Italians used the Cannone da 149/23, designed and built by Gio. Ansaldo & C. It was introduced in 1882 and lasted until 1918, it's main purpose was aid during sieges and also serve as coastal artillery, being mounted in steel gun turrets. \n\nLater, the Italians introduced the Obice da 149/12, produced by Ansaldo and Vickers Terni, it entered service in 1914, having 3 different versions during the service in WW1, all of them being widely used by the Italian Army. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. The French introduced the Mortier de 150 mm T Mle 1917 during the last years of WW1, it was used until 1940, some of them participated during the Phony War, for transport it was broken down into three loads. \n\nIn the East, the Japanese designed the Type 89 15cm cannon, being the main gun of their heavy artillery units. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. {khi_150mm_descr_short}Heavy artillery that has a massive range and fires big projectiles, very useful for sieges.

{khi_5lb}Mortar
{khi_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{khi_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.

{khi_gatling}Gatling Gun
{khi_gatling_descr}"It occurred to me that if I could invent a machine which could by its rapidity of fire, enable one man to do as much battle duty as a hundred, that it would, to a large extent supersede the necessity of large armies, and consequently, exposure to battle and disease be greatly diminished" - Richard J. Gatling \n\nThe Gatling Gun was invented in 1861, during the American Civil War, by Richard Jordan Gatling (Hertford County, 1818 - New York City, 1903). It was the first completely reliable machinegun of the era, combining a good firing rate, accuracy and an easy way to reload it. It uses a cyclic multi-barrel system that rotates clockwise and facilitates the cooling, aslo synchronizing the firing-reloading sequence. \n\nRichard Gatling invented the weapon to minimize the disease casualties of the American Civil War, however, the Gatling Gun was barely used by the Union forces because of the fear caused by such devastating gun. The Union forces used them during the siege of Petersburg (June 1864 - April 1865), it was later accepted by the army in 1866 after a demonstration made by a representative. The major European powers purchased and vastly used Gatling Guns for their colonial expansions, being the British the most well known for using them during the Anglo-Ashanti Wars and the Anglo-Zulu War. During the Boshin War, the Imperial Forces of Japan used some Gatling Guns; they were also vastly used during the War of the Pacific by the Peruvian and Chilean forces, in both Navy and Army, being especially used in the battle of Alto de la Alianza (1880) and the battle of San Juan y Chorrillos (1881). Later during the Spanish-American War, the US forces used few Gatling Guns, but in an effective way, against the Spanish forces in the battle of San Juan Hill (1898); however, the jungle footpaths and other incovenients were a problem for them, and most of the US forces were already using modern 1895 Browning Machineguns. \n\nThe Gatling Gun was rapidly displaced by other modern machineguns, and the US Army declared them obsolete in 1911. However, later, the mechanism and systems of the Gatling Gun were revived and reused to invent Miniguns and the famous M61 Vulcano.
{khi_gatling_descr_short}Early Machineguns that deal massive damage to close formations and have decent range.

{khi_maxim}Maxim Gun
{khi_maxim_descr}The Maxim Gun was invented by Sir Hiram Stevens Maxim in 1884, with a modern recoil-operated firing system, it was called "the weapon most associated with imperial conquest" by the historian Martin Gilbert, since it was vastly used in the Scramble for Africa by the colonial powers. It inspired the developement of more machineguns, the Maxim featured a water cooling, that would maintain it's rate of fire far longer than other guns, it could fire 600 rounds per minute. However, it was heavy, and would need a group of men to rapidly recharge and pour water to maintain the firing rate. \n\nMaxim established his own company to produce his guns, financied by Albert Vickers. The first patents related to the developement of the Maxim gun were registered in June and July of 1883. The Maxim Gun saw first use during the Scramble for Africa, mainly for it's psychological effect on the enemy. It was used extensively during the First Matabele War in Rhodesia. It devastated entire African armies because of their obsolete charging tactics. The military authorities of the era rejected Machineguns because of their tendency to jam. However, the Maxim gun was a lot more reliable than its contemporaries. \n\nThe Maxim Gun saw problems because of the smoke that firing it caused, however, many people (including Hiram's brother, Hudson Maxim) developed smokeless powder to solve this problem. The British adopted them under the guidance of Sir Garnet Wolseley, he had a good reputation and love for innovative ideas. Maxim's design was also purchased and used by several European countries. Both Japanese and Russian forces used it during the Russo-Japanese War. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, most of the European countries adopted more modern machineguns; the British Vickers MG was an improved and redesigned Maxim, while the German Maschinengewehr 08 and the Russian Pulemyot Maxim were more direct copies of the original Maxim gun. The Maxim was also used during the Russian Civil War, some depictions include it being mounted in Tachankas. After that, the Maxim's variants stayed in service during WW2 and the first decades of the Cold War.
{khi_maxim_descr_short}Late Machinegun that improves the range and uses a new recoil-operated firing system, effective against large number of enemies.

The 15cm L/40 Feldkanone also appeared in WW1, the Germans were desperate for long-range artillery, so they designed this new heavy artillery, it was mainly used in rails and mounted in platforms, however, land versions existed. The Italians used the Cannone da 149/23, designed and built by Gio. Ansaldo & C. It was introduced in 1882 and lasted until 1918, it's main purpose was aid during sieges and also serve as coastal artillery, being mounted in steel gun turrets. \n\nLater, the Italians introduced the Obice da 149/12, produced by Ansaldo and Vickers Terni, it entered service in 1914, having 3 different versions during the service in WW1, all of them being widely used by the Italian Army. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. The French introduced the Mortier de 150 mm T Mle 1917 during the last years of WW1, it was used until 1940, some of them participated during the Phony War, for transport it was broken down into three loads. \n\nIn the East, the Japanese designed the Type 89 15cm cannon, being the main gun of their heavy artillery units. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. 
{kok_150mm_descr_short}Heavy artillery that has a massive range and fires big projectiles, very useful for sieges.

{kok_5lb}Mortar
{kok_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{kok_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.

{kok_armstrong_descr}Old Bronze Cannon firing solid shot

{kok_gatling}Gatling Gun
{kok_gatling_descr}"It occurred to me that if I could invent a machine which could by its rapidity of fire, enable one man to do as much battle duty as a hundred, that it would, to a large extent supersede the necessity of large armies, and consequently, exposure to battle and disease be greatly diminished" - Richard J. Gatling \n\nThe Gatling Gun was invented in 1861, during the American Civil War, by Richard Jordan Gatling (Hertford County, 1818 - New York City, 1903). It was the first completely reliable machinegun of the era, combining a good firing rate, accuracy and an easy way to reload it. It uses a cyclic multi-barrel system that rotates clockwise and facilitates the cooling, aslo synchronizing the firing-reloading sequence. \n\nRichard Gatling invented the weapon to minimize the disease casualties of the American Civil War, however, the Gatling Gun was barely used by the Union forces because of the fear caused by such devastating gun. The Union forces used them during the siege of Petersburg (June 1864 - April 1865), it was later accepted by the army in 1866 after a demonstration made by a representative. The major European powers purchased and vastly used Gatling Guns for their colonial expansions, being the British the most well known for using them during the Anglo-Ashanti Wars and the Anglo-Zulu War. During the Boshin War, the Imperial Forces of Japan used some Gatling Guns; they were also vastly used during the War of the Pacific by the Peruvian and Chilean forces, in both Navy and Army, being especially used in the battle of Alto de la Alianza (1880) and the battle of San Juan y Chorrillos (1881). Later during the Spanish-American War, the US forces used few Gatling Guns, but in an effective way, against the Spanish forces in the battle of San Juan Hill (1898); however, the jungle footpaths and other incovenients were a problem for them, and most of the US forces were already using modern 1895 Browning Machineguns. \n\nThe Gatling Gun was rapidly displaced by other modern machineguns, and the US Army declared them obsolete in 1911. However, later, the mechanism and systems of the Gatling Gun were revived and reused to invent Miniguns and the famous M61 Vulcano.

{kok_gatling_descr_short}Gatling Gun (Machine Gun, Gatling Model 1865), hand-cranked rapid-fire artillery.

{kok_inf}Sarbazlar (Late)
{kok_inf_descr}Russian-trained regular infantry with Berdan II rifles.
{kok_inf_descr_short}Sarbazlar (Rifle, Berdan II), Russian-trained regular infantry with Berdan II rifles.

{kok_maxim}Maxim Gun
{kok_maxim_descr}The Maxim Gun was invented by Sir Hiram Stevens Maxim in 1884, with a modern recoil-operated firing system, it was called "the weapon most associated with imperial conquest" by the historian Martin Gilbert, since it was vastly used in the Scramble for Africa by the colonial powers. It inspired the developement of more machineguns, the Maxim featured a water cooling, that would maintain it's rate of fire far longer than other guns, it could fire 600 rounds per minute. However, it was heavy, and would need a group of men to rapidly recharge and pour water to maintain the firing rate. \n\nMaxim established his own company to produce his guns, financied by Albert Vickers. The first patents related to the developement of the Maxim gun were registered in June and July of 1883. The Maxim Gun saw first use during the Scramble for Africa, mainly for it's psychological effect on the enemy. It was used extensively during the First Matabele War in Rhodesia. It devastated entire African armies because of their obsolete charging tactics. The military authorities of the era rejected Machineguns because of their tendency to jam. However, the Maxim gun was a lot more reliable than its contemporaries. \n\nThe Maxim Gun saw problems because of the smoke that firing it caused, however, many people (including Hiram's brother, Hudson Maxim) developed smokeless powder to solve this problem. The British adopted them under the guidance of Sir Garnet Wolseley, he had a good reputation and love for innovative ideas. Maxim's design was also purchased and used by several European countries. Both Japanese and Russian forces used it during the Russo-Japanese War. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, most of the European countries adopted more modern machineguns; the British Vickers MG was an improved and redesigned Maxim, while the German Maschinengewehr 08 and the Russian Pulemyot Maxim were more direct copies of the original Maxim gun. The Maxim was also used during the Russian Civil War, some depictions include it being mounted in Tachankas. After that, the Maxim's variants stayed in service during WW2 and the first decades of the Cold War.
{kok_maxim_descr_short}Late Machinegun that improves the range and uses a new recoil-operated firing system, effective against large number of enemies.

Though slower than later steel warships, they proved nearly invulnerable to older naval guns. Their appearance marked the beginning of a new era upon the world's oceans.

{mahdist_camel_cav}Mahdist Camelry (Carbine, Remington No. 1 Rolling Block)
{mahdist_camel_cav_descr}Mahdist Camelry (Harquebus)  By 1895 the Mahdists had a large number of captured rifles in their inventory. Khalifa Abdallahi ibn Muhammad, who succeeded Muhammad Ahmad al-Mahdi as head of the Mahdist state in 1885, was open to the idea of the use of firearms if it meant the continued survival of the jihad. To this end he created a few units of Riflemen and cavalry. They were armed with Remington Rolling Block rifles, and given sufficient amounts of ammunition. Captured Egyptian army officers were employed to instruct them on how to use these weapons properly. These units were prized by the Khalifa, and always kept in and around the Mahdist capital of Khartoum.

{mus_150mm}Howitzers 
{mus_150mm_descr}The 150 MM Guns (15 cm) have a ton of variations, being used by many countries around the world. These guns have a very high range compared to other artillery and fire bigger shells than regular artillery, serving for siege and defensive purposes mainly. The Germans developed the 15cm Kanone 16, used during WW1 and WW2, it would fire 2 types of highly explosive shells and served mainly for coastal defense during WW2. The 15cm L/40 Feldkanone also appeared in WW1, the Germans were desperate for long-range artillery, so they designed this new heavy artillery, it was mainly used in rails and mounted in platforms, however, land versions existed. The Italians used the Cannone da 149/23, designed and built by Gio. Ansaldo & C. It was introduced in 1882 and lasted until 1918, it's main purpose was aid during sieges and also serve as coastal artillery, being mounted in steel gun turrets. \n\nLater, the Italians introduced the Obice da 149/12, produced by Ansaldo and Vickers Terni, it entered service in 1914, having 3 different versions during the service in WW1, all of them being widely used by the Italian Army. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. The French introduced the Mortier de 150 mm T Mle 1917 during the last years of WW1, it was used until 1940, some of them participated during the Phony War, for transport it was broken down into three loads. \n\nIn the East, the Japanese designed the Type 89 15cm cannon, being the main gun of their heavy artillery units. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. The Wehrmatch used the 15 cm sFH 18 as their main howitzer, it had great success and was the first artillery that introduced rocket-assisted ammunition, replacing th
{mus_150mm_descr_short}Heavy artillery that has a massive range and fires big projectiles, very useful for sieges.

{mus_5lb}Mortar
{mus_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{mus_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.

{mus_gatling}Gatling Gun 
{mus_gatling_descr}"It occurred to me that if I could invent a machine which could by its rapidity of fire, enable one man to do as much battle duty as a hundred, that it would, to a large extent supersede the necessity of large armies, and consequently, exposure to battle and disease be greatly diminished" - Richard J. Gatling \n\nThe Gatling Gun was invented in 1861, during the American Civil War, by Richard Jordan Gatling (Hertford County, 1818 - New York City, 1903). It was the first completely reliable machinegun of the era, combining a good firing rate, accuracy and an easy way to reload it. It uses a cyclic multi-barrel system that rotates clockwise and facilitates the cooling, aslo synchronizing the firing-reloading sequence. \n\nRichard Gatling invented the weapon to minimize the disease casualties of the American Civil War, however, the Gatling Gun was barely used by the Union forces because of the fear caused by such devastating gun. The Union forces used them during the siege of Petersburg (June 1864 - April 1865), it was later accepted by the army in 1866 after a demonstration made by a representative. The major European powers purchased and vastly used Gatling Guns for their colonial expansions, being the British the most well known for using them during the Anglo-Ashanti Wars and the Anglo-Zulu War. During the Boshin War, the Imperial Forces of Japan used some Gatling Guns; they were also vastly used during the War of the Pacific by the Peruvian and Chilean forces, in both Navy and Army, being especially used in the battle of Alto de la Alianza (1880) and the battle of San Juan y Chorrillos (1881). Later during the Spanish-American War, the US forces used few Gatling Guns, but in an effective way, against the Spanish forces in the battle of San Juan Hill (1898); however, the jungle footpaths and other incovenients were a problem for them, and most of the US forces were already using modern 1895 Browning Machineguns. \n\nThe Gatling Gun was rapidly displaced by other modern machineguns, and the US Army declared them obsolete in 1911. However, later, the mechanism and systems of the Gatling Gun were revived and reused to invent Miniguns and the famous M61 Vulcano.
{mus_gatling_descr_short}Early Machineguns that deal massive damage to close formations and have decent range.

{rus_12lb}Muzzle-loading Rifled Cannon
{rus_12lb_descr}Generally the 12 pounder guns during the early years of the XIXth Century were muzzle-loading and easy to manouver, being the 12 pounder Armstrong Gun a revolutionary one that featured an easier way to reload it, the breech-loading system, that greatly increased the firing rate. The Withworth gun was used by the US and Confederate forces, but it was considered rare. The Canon obusier de 12 was a heavy howitzer developed in France in 1853 and saw a vast use during the American Civil War by both Union and Confederate armies. The Obusier de 12 was rapidly adopted because of their variation of ammunition; despite being muzzle-loading, it was light enough to be transported rapidly on the field, strong enough to destroy heavy fortifications almost a mile away, and versatile enough to fire shells, balls or canister. \n\nMost of the cannons of the Union and the Confederate forces were 12 pounder Napoleon Model 1857; the Union manufactured over 1100 cannons while the Confederacy manufactured over 600 cannons. It was the last cast bronze gun used by the American Army; in the battle of Gettysburg, 142 of the 360 Union cannons were Napoleons. \n\nThe 12 pounder Armstrong was invented in Britain by William G. Armstrong and it was the first breech-loading 12 pounder gun in the world, it saw use in New Zealand during the First and Second Taranaki War. It was used by both the British Army and the Royal Navy, however, William Armstrong received a lot of complaints about his guns, so he retired from service in 1862 and Britain returned to the use of muzzle-loaders. Another reason of the displacement of the Armstrong gun was the cost of maintainment and the hard training of the gunners. \n\nLater, the British adopted new breech-loading cannons in 1885, the Ordnance BL 12-pounder 7 cwt, used first by the Royal Field Artillery and the Royal Horse Artillery, however, it was hard to transport by horses because of it's weight, so in 1892 the Royal Horse Artillery received a new 12 pounder 6 cwt gun. These cannons were in service until 1895, at that point the 12-pounder 7 cwt became redundant. The Royal Navy adopted the QF 12-pounder 8 cwt gun in 1894, it was used in the early stages of the Second Boer War and in the West and East Africa campaigns of WW1. In WW1 the British Army adopted the QF 12-pounder 12 cwt AA Gun, it was introduced after the German invasion of Belgium and northern France, because the British expected air attacks; it was usually used to defend dockyards and river barges (in Mesopotamia).
{rus_12lb_descr_short}Muzzle-loading rifled cannons that have a good range and can inflict good damage against tight formations, they can also use grapeshot.

{rus_150mm}Howitzers
{rus_150mm_descr}The 150 MM Guns (15 cm) have a ton of variations, being used by many countries around the world. These guns have a very high range compared to other artillery and fire bigger shells than regular artillery, serving for siege and defensive purposes mainly. The Germans developed the 15cm Kanone 16, used during WW1 and WW2, it would fire 2 types of highly explosive shells and served mainly for coastal defense during WW2. The 15cm L/40 Feldkanone also appeared in WW1, the Germans were desperate for long-range artillery, so they designed this new heavy artillery, it was mainly used in rails and mounted in platforms, however, land versions existed. The Italians used the Cannone da 149/23, designed and built by Gio. Ansaldo & C. It was introduced in 1882 and lasted until 1918, it's main purpose was aid during sieges and also serve as coastal artillery, being mounted in steel gun turrets. \n\nLater, the Italians introduced the Obice da 149/12, produced by Ansaldo and Vickers Terni, it entered service in 1914, having 3 different versions during the service in WW1, all of them being widely used by the Italian Army. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. The French introduced the Mortier de 150 mm T Mle 1917 during the last years of WW1, it was used until 1940, some of them participated during the Phony War, for transport it was broken down into three loads. \n\nIn the East, the Japanese designed the Type 89 15cm cannon, being the main gun of their heavy artillery units. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. 
{rus_150mm_descr_short}Heavy artillery that has a massive range and fires big projectiles, very useful for sieges.

{rus_5lb}Mortar
{rus_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{rus_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.

{rus_maxim}Maxim Gun
{rus_maxim_descr}The Maxim Gun was invented by Sir Hiram Stevens Maxim in 1884, with a modern recoil-operated firing system, it was called "the weapon most associated with imperial conquest" by the historian Martin Gilbert, since it was vastly used in the Scramble for Africa by the colonial powers. It inspired the developement of more machineguns, the Maxim featured a water cooling, that would maintain it's rate of fire far longer than other guns, it could fire 600 rounds per minute. However, it was heavy, and would need a group of men to rapidly recharge and pour water to maintain the firing rate. \n\nMaxim established his own company to produce his guns, financied by Albert Vickers. The first patents related to the developement of the Maxim gun were registered in June and July of 1883. The Maxim Gun saw first use during the Scramble for Africa, mainly for it's psychological effect on the enemy. It was used extensively during the First Matabele War in Rhodesia. It devastated entire African armies because of their obsolete charging tactics. The military authorities of the era rejected Machineguns because of their tendency to jam. However, the Maxim gun was a lot more reliable than its contemporaries. \n\nThe Maxim Gun saw problems because of the smoke that firing it caused, however, many people (including Hiram's brother, Hudson Maxim) developed smokeless powder to solve this problem. The British adopted them under the guidance of Sir Garnet Wolseley, he had a good reputation and love for innovative ideas. Maxim's design was also purchased and used by several European countries. Both Japanese and Russian forces used it during the Russo-Japanese War. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, most of the European countries adopted more modern machineguns; the British Vickers MG was an improved and redesigned Maxim, while the German Maschinengewehr 08 and the Russian Pulemyot Maxim were more direct copies of the original Maxim gun. The Maxim was also used during the Russian Civil War, some depictions include it being mounted in Tachankas. After that, the Maxim's variants stayed in service during WW2 and the first decades of the Cold War.
{rus_maxim_descr_short}Late Machinegun that improves the range and uses a new recoil-operated firing system, effective against large number of enemies.
