{ship_armoured_cruiser}Armoured Cruiser
{ship_armoured_cruiser_descr}Armoured Cruisers are large, powerful warships designed to combine battleship protection with the speed and endurance of a cruiser. Their armoured belts and heavy guns allow them to destroy lighter vessels, protect trade routes, and reinforce the battle line. They are expensive ships, but their ability to operate independently across great distances makes them valuable instruments of imperial power. Only true battleships can reliably defeat them in a direct engagement.
{ship_armoured_cruiser_descr_short}Large armoured cruisers capable of hunting lesser ships and supporting the main battle fleet.

{ship_armoured_frigate}Armoured Frigate
{ship_armoured_frigate_descr}Armoured Frigates combine the cruising qualities of a frigate with iron protection strong enough to resist much contemporary naval gunfire. Their heavy batteries allow them to defeat lighter ships and threaten older wooden warships with ease. Although faster than most battleships, the weight of their armour reduces manoeuvrability and places great demands upon their engines. They are powerful ocean-going warships capable of operating far from friendly ports.
{ship_armoured_frigate_descr_short}Ocean-going frigates protected by iron armour and armed to overpower lighter warships.

{ship_composite_corvette}Composite Corvette
{ship_composite_corvette_descr}Composite Corvettes combine iron framing with wooden planking to produce stronger and more durable hulls than earlier warships. Designed for lengthy overseas deployments, they require less maintenance while retaining the advantages of traditional wooden construction. Their respectable speed and balanced armament make them excellent patrol vessels across the far reaches of an empire. They represent an important step towards fully iron-built navies.
{ship_composite_corvette_descr_short}Hybrid warships with iron frames and wooden hulls, built for long-range patrol and escort duties.

{ship_destroyer}Torpedo Boat Destroyer
{ship_destroyer_descr}Torpedo Boat Destroyers were developed to counter the growing threat posed by fast torpedo craft. Combining high speed with rapid-firing guns and improved seaworthiness, they can intercept enemy raiders before they reach the battle fleet. As naval technology advances, these versatile vessels increasingly take on scouting and escort duties alongside their original mission. They are the forerunners of the modern destroyer.
{ship_destroyer_descr_short}Torpedo Boat Destroyer

{ship_iron_frigate}Iron-hulled Frigate
{ship_iron_frigate_descr}Iron-hulled Frigates replace vulnerable wooden construction with stronger iron frames and plating. Their improved hulls support heavier machinery and armament while offering greater resistance to fire and splintering. Designed for long patrols and independent service, they can protect distant trade routes or hunt enemy raiders across the oceans. They remain lighter and faster than battleships but cannot withstand the heaviest naval guns.
{ship_iron_frigate_descr_short}Iron-hulled Frigate

{ship_ironclad_corvette}Ironclad Ram
{ship_ironclad_corvette_descr}The Ironclad Ram reflects a brief but influential period in naval thought when heavily armoured ships were expected to sink their enemies by ramming. Reinforced bows, iron armour, and heavy guns allow these vessels to fight at close quarters with terrifying effect. Although changing naval tactics gradually reduced the importance of ramming, these ships remain dangerous opponents in confined waters. Few captains willingly face one head-on.
{ship_ironclad_corvette_descr_short}Ironclad Ram

{ship_monitor}Monitor
{ship_monitor_descr}Monitors are heavily armoured warships mounting one or two enormous guns within revolving turrets. Built with an exceptionally low profile, they present a difficult target while delivering crushing firepower against coastal fortifications and enemy vessels. Their limited seaworthiness confines them largely to coastal waters and rivers, where their heavy armour provides a decisive advantage. They are among the most intimidating ships in littoral warfare.
{ship_monitor_descr_short}Monitor

{ship_pre_dreadnought}Pre-Dreadnought
{ship_pre_dreadnought_descr}Pre-Dreadnoughts are heavily armoured battleships built around a small number of powerful main guns supported by numerous secondary weapons. Their steel hulls and thick armour allow them to endure punishment that would cripple lesser vessels. Although formidable in the battle line, their mixed batteries complicate long-range gunnery and limit the effectiveness of concentrated fire. They represent the height of naval power before the arrival of the all-big-gun battleship.
{ship_pre_dreadnought_descr_short}Pre-Dreadnought

{ship_protected_cruiser}Protected Cruiser
{ship_protected_cruiser_descr}Protected Cruisers rely upon an armoured deck to shield their engines, magazines, and other vital machinery from plunging fire. Lighter than armoured cruisers, they possess the speed and endurance needed to patrol colonies, escort merchant shipping, and hunt enemy raiders. Their rapid-firing guns can overwhelm smaller vessels, but the absence of heavy side armour leaves them vulnerable in a sustained duel. Their versatility makes them valuable additions to any modern fleet.
{ship_protected_cruiser_descr_short}Protected Cruiser

{ship_sail_corvette}Wooden Sail Corvette
{ship_sail_corvette_descr}Wooden Sail Corvettes are small and economical warships used for patrols, dispatch duty, coastal defence, and the protection of merchant traffic. Carrying fewer guns than frigates, they depend upon agility and seamanship when confronting hostile vessels. Their shallow draught allows them to operate in waters too confined for larger ships. Though unsuited to fleet battles, they remain useful on distant stations and colonial coastlines.
{ship_sail_corvette_descr_short}Wooden Sail Corvette

{ship_sail_sotl}Ship of the Line
{ship_sail_sotl_descr}The Ship of the Line represents the final and most powerful expression of the great wooden battle fleets that once ruled the seas. Mounting multiple gun decks filled with heavy cannon, these enormous warships deliver devastating broadsides capable of smashing almost any opponent afloat. Though entirely dependent upon favourable winds, their firepower remains formidable even as steam begins to transform naval warfare. They are floating fortresses worthy of the proudest naval traditions.
{ship_sail_sotl_descr_short}Sail Ship of the Line

{ship_steam_corvette}Wooden Steam Corvette
{ship_steam_corvette_descr}Wooden Steam Corvettes combine the simplicity of traditional corvettes with the reliability of steam propulsion. Fast, economical, and capable of operating independently, they are ideal for colonial patrols, escorting merchant convoys, and suppressing piracy. Though lightly armed compared to larger warships, their versatility ensures they remain valuable throughout the age of transition. They are dependable vessels for nations building modern navies on limited budgets.
{ship_steam_corvette_descr_short}Wooden Steam Corvette

{sho_12lb}Muzzle-Loading Rifled Cannon
{sho_12lb_descr}Generally the 12 pounder guns during the early years of the XIXth Century were muzzle-loading and easy to manouver, being the 12 pounder Armstrong Gun a revolutionary one that featured an easier way to reload it, the breech-loading system, that greatly increased the firing rate. The Withworth gun was used by the US and Confederate forces, but it was considered rare. The Canon obusier de 12 was a heavy howitzer developed in France in 1853 and saw a vast use during the American Civil War by both Union and Confederate armies. The Obusier de 12 was rapidly adopted because of their variation of ammunition; despite being muzzle-loading, it was light enough to be transported rapidly on the field, strong enough to destroy heavy fortifications almost a mile away, and versatile enough to fire shells, balls or canister. \n\nMost of the cannons of the Union and the Confederate forces were 12 pounder Napoleon Model 1857; the Union manufactured over 1100 cannons while the Confederacy manufactured over 600 cannons. It was the last cast bronze gun used by the American Army; in the battle of Gettysburg, 142 of the 360 Union cannons were Napoleons. \n\nThe 12 pounder Armstrong was invented in Britain by William G. Armstrong and it was the first breech-loading 12 pounder gun in the world, it saw use in New Zealand during the First and Second Taranaki War. It was used by both the British Army and the Royal Navy, however, William Armstrong received a lot of complaints about his guns, so he retired from service in 1862 and Britain returned to the use of muzzle-loaders. Another reason of the displacement of the Armstrong gun was the cost of maintainment and the hard training of the gunners. \n\nLater, the British adopted new breech-loading cannons in 1885, the Ordnance BL 12-pounder 7 cwt, used first by the Royal Field Artillery and the Royal Horse Artillery, however, it was hard to transport by horses because of it's weight, so in 1892 the Royal Horse Artillery received a new 12 pounder 6 cwt gun. These cannons were in service until 1895, at that point the 12-pounder 7 cwt became redundant. The Royal Navy adopted the QF 12-pounder 8 cwt gun in 1894, it was used in the early stages of the Second Boer War and in the West and East Africa campaigns of WW1. In WW1 the British Army adopted the QF 12-pounder 12 cwt AA Gun, it was introduced after the German invasion of Belgium and northern France, because the British expected air attacks; it was usually used to defend dockyards and river barges (in Mesopotamia).
{sho_12lb_descr_short}Muzzle-loading rifled cannons that have a good range and can inflict good damage against tight formations, they can also use grapeshot.\nTOKUGAWA ONLY

{sho_150mm}Howitzers 
{sho_150mm_descr}The 150 MM Guns (15 cm) have a ton of variations, being used by many countries around the world. These guns have a very high range compared to other artillery and fire bigger shells than regular artillery, serving for siege and defensive purposes mainly. It was introduced in 1882 and lasted until 1918, it's main purpose was aid during sieges and also serve as coastal artillery, being mounted in steel gun turrets. \n\nLater, the Italians introduced the Obice da 149/12, produced by Ansaldo and Vickers Terni, it entered service in 1914, having 3 different versions during the service in WW1, all of them being widely used by the Italian Army. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. The French introduced the Mortier de 150 mm T Mle 1917 during the last years of WW1, it was used until 1940, some of them participated during the Phony War, for transport it was broken down into three loads. \n\nIn the East, the Japanese designed the Type 89 15cm cannon, being the main gun of their heavy artillery units. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. {sho_5lb}Mortar
{sho_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{sho_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.\nTOKUGAWA ONLY

{sho_gatling}Gatling Gun
{sho_gatling_descr}"It occurred to me that if I could invent a machine which could by its rapidity of fire, enable one man to do as much battle duty as a hundred, that it would, to a large extent supersede the necessity of large armies, and consequently, exposure to battle and disease be greatly diminished" - Richard J. Gatling \n\nThe Gatling Gun was invented in 1861, during the American Civil War, by Richard Jordan Gatling (Hertford County, 1818 - New York City, 1903). It was the first completely reliable machinegun of the era, combining a good firing rate, accuracy and an easy way to reload it. It uses a cyclic multi-barrel system that rotates clockwise and facilitates the cooling, aslo synchronizing the firing-reloading sequence. \n\nRichard Gatling invented the weapon to minimize the disease casualties of the American Civil War, however, the Gatling Gun was barely used by the Union forces because of the fear caused by such devastating gun. The Union forces used them during the siege of Petersburg (June 1864 - April 1865), it was later accepted by the army in 1866 after a demonstration made by a representative. The major European powers purchased and vastly used Gatling Guns for their colonial expansions, being the British the most well known for using them during the Anglo-Ashanti Wars and the Anglo-Zulu War. During the Boshin War, the Imperial Forces of Japan used some Gatling Guns; they were also vastly used during the War of the Pacific by the Peruvian and Chilean forces, in both Navy and Army, being especially used in the battle of Alto de la Alianza (1880) and the battle of San Juan y Chorrillos (1881). Later during the Spanish-American War, the US forces used few Gatling Guns, but in an effective way, against the Spanish forces in the battle of San Juan Hill (1898); however, the jungle footpaths and other incovenients were a problem for them, and most of the US forces were already using modern 1895 Browning Machineguns. \n\nThe Gatling Gun was rapidly displaced by other modern machineguns, and the US Army declared them obsolete in 1911. However, later, the mechanism and systems of the Gatling Gun were revived and reused to invent Miniguns and the famous M61 Vulcano.
{sho_gatling_descr_short}Early Machineguns that deal massive damage to close formations and have decent range.\nTOKUGAWA ONLY

{sho_maxim}Maxim Gun
{sho_maxim_descr}The Maxim Gun was invented by Sir Hiram Stevens Maxim in 1884, with a modern recoil-operated firing system, it was called "the weapon most associated with imperial conquest" by the historian Martin Gilbert, since it was vastly used in the Scramble for Africa by the colonial powers. It inspired the developement of more machineguns, the Maxim featured a water cooling, that would maintain it's rate of fire far longer than other guns, it could fire 600 rounds per minute. However, it was heavy, and would need a group of men to rapidly recharge and pour water to maintain the firing rate. \n\nMaxim established his own company to produce his guns, financied by Albert Vickers. The first patents related to the developement of the Maxim gun were registered in June and July of 1883. The Maxim Gun saw first use during the Scramble for Africa, mainly for it's psychological effect on the enemy. It was used extensively during the First Matabele War in Rhodesia. It devastated entire African armies because of their obsolete charging tactics. The military authorities of the era rejected Machineguns because of their tendency to jam. However, the Maxim gun was a lot more reliable than its contemporaries. \n\nThe Maxim Gun saw problems because of the smoke that firing it caused, however, many people (including Hiram's brother, Hudson Maxim) developed smokeless powder to solve this problem. The British adopted them under the guidance of Sir Garnet Wolseley, he had a good reputation and love for innovative ideas. Maxim's design was also purchased and used by several European countries. Both Japanese and Russian forces used it during the Russo-Japanese War. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, most of the European countries adopted more modern machineguns; the British Vickers MG was an improved and redesigned Maxim, while the German Maschinengewehr 08 and the Russian Pulemyot Maxim were more direct copies of the original Maxim gun. The Maxim was also used during the Russian Civil War, some depictions include it being mounted in Tachankas. After that, the Maxim's variants stayed in service during WW2 and the first decades of the Cold War.
{sho_maxim_descr_short}Late Machinegun that improves the range and uses a new recoil-operated firing system, effective against large number of enemies.\nTOKUGAWA ONLY

{usa_12lb}Muzzle-loading Rifled Cannon
{usa_12lb_descr}Generally the 12 pounder guns during the early years of the XIXth Century were muzzle-loading and easy to manouver, being the 12 pounder Armstrong Gun a revolutionary one that featured an easier way to reload it, the breech-loading system, that greatly increased the firing rate. The Withworth gun was used by the US and Confederate forces, but it was considered rare. The Canon obusier de 12 was a heavy howitzer developed in France in 1853 and saw a vast use during the American Civil War by both Union and Confederate armies. The Obusier de 12 was rapidly adopted because of their variation of ammunition; despite being muzzle-loading, it was light enough to be transported rapidly on the field, strong enough to destroy heavy fortifications almost a mile away, and versatile enough to fire shells, balls or canister. \n\nMost of the cannons of the Union and the Confederate forces were 12 pounder Napoleon Model 1857; the Union manufactured over 1100 cannons while the Confederacy manufactured over 600 cannons. It was the last cast bronze gun used by the American Army; in the battle of Gettysburg, 142 of the 360 Union cannons were Napoleons. \n\nThe 12 pounder Armstrong was invented in Britain by William G. Armstrong and it was the first breech-loading 12 pounder gun in the world, it saw use in New Zealand during the First and Second Taranaki War. It was used by both the British Army and the Royal Navy, however, William Armstrong received a lot of complaints about his guns, so he retired from service in 1862 and Britain returned to the use of muzzle-loaders. Another reason of the displacement of the Armstrong gun was the cost of maintainment and the hard training of the gunners. \n\nLater, the British adopted new breech-loading cannons in 1885, the Ordnance BL 12-pounder 7 cwt, used first by the Royal Field Artillery and the Royal Horse Artillery, however, it was hard to transport by horses because of it's weight, so in 1892 the Royal Horse Artillery received a new 12 pounder 6 cwt gun. These cannons were in service until 1895, at that point the 12-pounder 7 cwt became redundant. The Royal Navy adopted the QF 12-pounder 8 cwt gun in 1894, it was used in the early stages of the Second Boer War and in the West and East Africa campaigns of WW1. In WW1 the British Army adopted the QF 12-pounder 12 cwt AA Gun, it was introduced after the German invasion of Belgium and northern France, because the British expected air attacks; it was usually used to defend dockyards and river barges (in Mesopotamia).
{usa_12lb_descr_short}Muzzle-loading rifled cannons that have a good range and can inflict good damage against tight formations, they can also use grapeshot.

{usa_150mm}Howitzers 
{usa_150mm_descr}The 150 MM Guns (15 cm) have a ton of variations, being used by many countries around the world. These guns have a very high range compared to other artillery and fire bigger shells than regular artillery, serving for siege and defensive purposes mainly. The Germans developed the 15cm Kanone 16, used during WW1 and WW2, it would fire 2 types of highly explosive shells and served mainly for coastal defense during WW2. The 15cm L/40 Feldkanone also appeared in WW1, the Germans were desperate for long-range artillery, so they designed this new heavy artillery, it was mainly used in rails and mounted in platforms, however, land versions existed. The Italians used the Cannone da 149/23, designed and built by Gio. Ansaldo & C. It was introduced in 1882 and lasted until 1918, it's main purpose was aid during sieges and also serve as coastal artillery, being mounted in steel gun turrets. \n\nLater, the Italians introduced the Obice da 149/12, produced by Ansaldo and Vickers Terni, it entered service in 1914, having 3 different versions during the service in WW1, all of them being widely used by the Italian Army. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. The French introduced the Mortier de 150 mm T Mle 1917 during the last years of WW1, it was used until 1940, some of them participated during the Phony War, for transport it was broken down into three loads. \n\nIn the East, the Japanese designed the Type 89 15cm cannon, being the main gun of their heavy artillery units. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. The Wehrmatch used the 15 cm sFH 18 as their main howitzer, it had great success and was the first artillery that introduced rocket-assisted ammunition, replacing th
{usa_150mm_descr_short}Heavy artillery that has a massive range and fires big projectiles, very useful for sieges.

{usa_5lb}Mortar
{usa_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{usa_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.

{usa_armstrong}Breech-Loading Rifled Cannon
{usa_armstrong_descr}Breech-Loading Rifled Cannon
{usa_armstrong_descr_short}Breech-Loading Rifled Cannon

{usa_gatling}Gatling Gun 
{usa_gatling_descr}"It occurred to me that if I could invent a machine which could by its rapidity of fire, enable one man to do as much battle duty as a hundred, that it would, to a large extent supersede the necessity of large armies, and consequently, exposure to battle and disease be greatly diminished" - Richard J. Gatling \n\nThe Gatling Gun was invented in 1861, during the American Civil War, by Richard Jordan Gatling (Hertford County, 1818 - New York City, 1903). It was the first completely reliable machinegun of the era, combining a good firing rate, accuracy and an easy way to reload it. It uses a cyclic multi-barrel system that rotates clockwise and facilitates the cooling, aslo synchronizing the firing-reloading sequence. \n\nRichard Gatling invented the weapon to minimize the disease casualties of the American Civil War, however, the Gatling Gun was barely used by the Union forces because of the fear caused by such devastating gun. The Union forces used them during the siege of Petersburg (June 1864 - April 1865), it was later accepted by the army in 1866 after a demonstration made by a representative. The major European powers purchased and vastly used Gatling Guns for their colonial expansions, being the British the most well known for using them during the Anglo-Ashanti Wars and the Anglo-Zulu War. During the Boshin War, the Imperial Forces of Japan used some Gatling Guns; they were also vastly used during the War of the Pacific by the Peruvian and Chilean forces, in both Navy and Army, being especially used in the battle of Alto de la Alianza (1880) and the battle of San Juan y Chorrillos (1881). Later during the Spanish-American War, the US forces used few Gatling Guns, but in an effective way, against the Spanish forces in the battle of San Juan Hill (1898); however, the jungle footpaths and other incovenients were a problem for them, and most of the US forces were already using modern 1895 Browning Machineguns. \n\nThe Gatling Gun was rapidly displaced by other modern machineguns, and the US Army declared them obsolete in 1911. However, later, the mechanism and systems of the Gatling Gun were revived and reused to invent Miniguns and the famous M61 Vulcano.
{usa_gatling_descr_short}Early Machineguns that deal massive damage to close formations and have decent range.

{usa_maxim}Maxim Gun
{usa_maxim_descr}The Maxim Gun was invented by Sir Hiram Stevens Maxim in 1884, with a modern recoil-operated firing system, it was called "the weapon most associated with imperial conquest" by the historian Martin Gilbert, since it was vastly used in the Scramble for Africa by the colonial powers. It inspired the developement of more machineguns, the Maxim featured a water cooling, that would maintain it's rate of fire far longer than other guns, it could fire 600 rounds per minute. However, it was heavy, and would need a group of men to rapidly recharge and pour water to maintain the firing rate. \n\nMaxim established his own company to produce his guns, financied by Albert Vickers. The first patents related to the developement of the Maxim gun were registered in June and July of 1883. The Maxim Gun saw first use during the Scramble for Africa, mainly for it's psychological effect on the enemy. It was used extensively during the First Matabele War in Rhodesia. It devastated entire African armies because of their obsolete charging tactics. The military authorities of the era rejected Machineguns because of their tendency to jam. However, the Maxim gun was a lot more reliable than its contemporaries. \n\nThe Maxim Gun saw problems because of the smoke that firing it caused, however, many people (including Hiram's brother, Hudson Maxim) developed smokeless powder to solve this problem. The British adopted them under the guidance of Sir Garnet Wolseley, he had a good reputation and love for innovative ideas. Maxim's design was also purchased and used by several European countries. Both Japanese and Russian forces used it during the Russo-Japanese War. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, most of the European countries adopted more modern machineguns; the British Vickers MG was an improved and redesigned Maxim, while the German Maschinengewehr 08 and the Russian Pulemyot Maxim were more direct copies of the original Maxim gun. The Maxim was also used during the Russian Civil War, some depictions include it being mounted in Tachankas. After that, the Maxim's variants stayed in service during WW2 and the first decades of the Cold War.
{usa_maxim_descr_short}Late Machinegun that improves the range and uses a new recoil-operated firing system, effective against large number of enemies.

These mountain dwellers although are armed with traditional melee weapons such as Dha swords and shields, their vast knowledge about their own geographic are allowed them to move from the mountainous area to another area very quickly than any other troops while using the ambush tactics that allowed them to give some surprise and fear-factor to its enemies that fought them inside their area.

These Vietnamese infantry, although armed with guns and having some training with marksmanship, still wore their traditional armor and clothes while having lower discipline and morale compared to any other western infantry troops. {zul_150mm}Howitzers 
{zul_150mm_descr}The 150 MM Guns (15 cm) have a ton of variations, being used by many countries around the world. These guns have a very high range compared to other artillery and fire bigger shells than regular artillery, serving for siege and defensive purposes mainly. The Germans developed the 15cm Kanone 16, used during WW1 and WW2, it would fire 2 types of highly explosive shells and served mainly for coastal defense during WW2. The 15cm L/40 Feldkanone also appeared in WW1, the Germans were desperate for long-range artillery, so they designed this new heavy artillery, it was mainly used in rails and mounted in platforms, however, land versions existed. The Italians used the Cannone da 149/23, designed and built by Gio. Ansaldo & C. It was introduced in 1882 and lasted until 1918, it's main purpose was aid during sieges and also serve as coastal artillery, being mounted in steel gun turrets. \n\nLater, the Italians introduced the Obice da 149/12, produced by Ansaldo and Vickers Terni, it entered service in 1914, having 3 different versions during the service in WW1, all of them being widely used by the Italian Army. They also served during WW2, being now exported to Albania and Bulgaria too. The French introduced the Mortier de 150 mm T Mle 1917 during the last years of WW1, it was used until 1940, some of them participated during the Phony War, for transport it was broken down into three loads. \n\nIn the East, the Japanese designed the Type 89 15cm cannon, being the main gun of their heavy artillery units. It was introduced in 1929 and lasted until 1945; the Japanese used bigger shells than the regular ones for a 15cm cannon, but they were satisfied about his siege role, however, in field, it was completely different, because of its size, it was transported in 2 loads and took longer to arm, it also had less range than the other nations cannons. The Wehrmatch used the 15 cm sFH 18 as their main howitzer, it had great success and was the first artillery that introduced rocket-assisted ammunition, replacing th
{zul_150mm_descr_short}Heavy artillery that has a massive range and fires big projectiles, very useful for sieges.

{zul_5lb}Mortar
{zul_5lb_descr}The term Howitzer originated from the Czech word "houfnice", the first appearance of the Howitzers was during the 16th Century, they were used mainly as siege weapons because of their superior range. During the 19th Century, most of the European armies started to adopt Howitzers as field and siege artillery, one of the most known guns is the 12 pounder Napoleon from France, vastly used by the US and CSA during the American Civil War. The rifling began to appear as an upgrade to normal artillery, the Howitzers seemed irrelevant and displaced by these new artillery, so rifled field cannons replaced both smoothbore field cannons and howitzers. However, in sieges, it was the opposite; rifled Howitzers had a larger impact against walls of fortified locations, so the generals used them for these circunstances. \n\nThere were 3 types of Howitzer present by the 1890s, the light siege howitzer (around 150mm of caliber), medium siege howitzers (around 200mm and 220mm of caliber) and heavy siege howitzers (around 240mm and 270mm of caliber). During the early years of the 20th Century, another category appeared, the "super-heavy siege howitzers", some of the best known are the German Big Bertha and the British Ordnance BL 15-inch Howitzer. These new howitzers would fire with more accuracy and cause severe damage against fortifications and troops protected by obstacles. \n\nBy the start of the Great War, the demand of Howitzers grow a lot, because of the necessity of guns that could fire in a more vertical trajectory to reach the trenches. The Howitzers developed during the war had longer barrels than the ones before the war; by the end of the war, the caracteristics of the guns and the howitzers were starting to be combined, high speed, long barrels, large range and good accuracy. \n\nDuring WW2, the Soviet forces (guided by their military doctrine) vastly used Howitzers on the Eastern Front, some examples are: the M-10 (152-mm Howitzer M1938), the M-30 (122-mm Howitzer M1938) and the D-1 (152.4-mm Howitzer M1943) In the present, the Howitzers combine the features of a field gun and a siege Howitzer; some nations like Russia call them "Gun-Howitzer", and most of the Commonwealth call them "Guns".
{zul_5lb_descr_short}Short barrel, large range and parabolic trajectory, these guns can inflict good damage at very long distances without risking to receive damage.

{zul_gatling}Gatling Gun
{zul_gatling_descr}"It occurred to me that if I could invent a machine which could by its rapidity of fire, enable one man to do as much battle duty as a hundred, that it would, to a large extent supersede the necessity of large armies, and consequently, exposure to battle and disease be greatly diminished" - Richard J. Gatling \n\nThe Gatling Gun was invented in 1861, during the American Civil War, by Richard Jordan Gatling (Hertford County, 1818 - New York City, 1903). It was the first completely reliable machinegun of the era, combining a good firing rate, accuracy and an easy way to reload it. It uses a cyclic multi-barrel system that rotates clockwise and facilitates the cooling, aslo synchronizing the firing-reloading sequence. \n\nRichard Gatling invented the weapon to minimize the disease casualties of the American Civil War, however, the Gatling Gun was barely used by the Union forces because of the fear caused by such devastating gun. The Union forces used them during the siege of Petersburg (June 1864 - April 1865), it was later accepted by the army in 1866 after a demonstration made by a representative. The major European powers purchased and vastly used Gatling Guns for their colonial expansions, being the British the most well known for using them during the Anglo-Ashanti Wars and the Anglo-Zulu War. During the Boshin War, the Imperial Forces of Japan used some Gatling Guns; they were also vastly used during the War of the Pacific by the Peruvian and Chilean forces, in both Navy and Army, being especially used in the battle of Alto de la Alianza (1880) and the battle of San Juan y Chorrillos (1881). Later during the Spanish-American War, the US forces used few Gatling Guns, but in an effective way, against the Spanish forces in the battle of San Juan Hill (1898); however, the jungle footpaths and other incovenients were a problem for them, and most of the US forces were already using modern 1895 Browning Machineguns. \n\nThe Gatling Gun was rapidly displaced by other modern machineguns, and the US Army declared them obsolete in 1911. However, later, the mechanism and systems of the Gatling Gun were revived and reused to invent Miniguns and the famous M61 Vulcano.
{zul_gatling_descr_short}Early Machineguns that deal massive damage to close formations and have decent range.

Riding swift horses across the African veld, they locate enemy movements, screen advancing armies, and relay vital intelligence to their commanders. {zulu_elite}iNduna Nezikhulu
{zulu_elite_descr}The Impi Commander and Officers lead the Zulu regiments with absolute authority, directing the famous "horns of the buffalo" formation from the front lines. Though firearms remain rare within the Impi, their trade muskets are often used to signal attacks and coordinate the advance of thousands of warriors. {zulu_guard}Amabutho amaZulu
{zulu_guard_descr}In the early 19th century, King Shaka decreed that Zulu men would not be allowed to marry until they had slain an enemy in battle. This fact essentially forced the bulk of young Zulu males to serve in the King's armies. Marriage allowed cattle ownership, something essential for independent livelihood in Zulu society. King Shaka also created an age based regimental system, and the cornerstone of the Zulu military became the Impis. Young Warrior Impi thus refers to an Impi comprised of young warriors, with the men typically being between 16 and 19 years old. Though inexperienced, their youth gives them an unparalleled tenacity and eagerness for battle.
{zulu_guard_descr_short}Amabutho amaZulu

{zulu_rifles}Ibutho Lezibhamu
{zulu_rifles_descr}Units of warriors armed with rifles acquired from dead British soldiers. Though armed with these very modern weapons, the Zulu didn't know how to aim them properly. Many believed that they were magical in nature, and that the bullets were basically capable of finding the targets on their own. Rifles were highly prized possessions by the Zulu, for obvious reasons, and warriors who were lucky enough to have them were expected to keep a great distance between themselves and the enemy. If threatened, the men in these units were expected to retreat, as this was preferable than letting the rifles to fall into (or, in the case of the British, return) to enemy hands.
{zulu_rifles_descr_short}Ibutho Lezibhamu (Rifle-Musket, Pattern 1853 Enfield)

{zulu_royals}Amabutho Amadala
{zulu_royals_descr}Men who had fought in multiple battles and who had killed at least 5 enemies were offered positions of leadership in Zulu armies. Upon reaching the age of 30, these elite warriors were given one of three options. First, retire from service with high honors and be given a plot of land in Ulundi near the King's residence. Second, continue to serve as a leader and mentor for the younger warriors. Third, fight in one of the elite units of the Zulu empire – the Elder Warrior Impis. Those that chose the third option were given uniforms made from leopard skins, which were believed to imbue them with divine power. Their flashy garb, which often includes ostrich feather headdresses, makes them stick out like a sore thumb on the battlefield. All the same they are extremely skilled warriors, and will likely fight to the last man if the situation calls for it.
{zulu_royals_descr_short}Amabutho Amadala

{zulu_spearmen}Amabutho amaXhosa
{zulu_spearmen_descr}Under the reign of King Mpande, who was King of the Zulu from 1840 to 1872, non Zulu men, including those from the Xhosa, Mfengu, and Basuto tribes, among others, were allowed to fight as warriors in Zulu armies. As non Zulu, these men would also have been classified as 'slaves'. Zulu society, like most African societies at the time, had slaves. These were not, however, like the slaves of the American South that we here in 21st century America think of when we hear this word. They weren't treated as objects or as farm animals. Rather, they were part of the 'slave class', a class at the very bottom of society. A good analogy might be the untouchables of India. Any village of non Zulus that was conquered by them would be made into a slave village. The people there would be allowed to stay, and even keep all their stuff, but the Zulu King held the right to conscript them for any purpose he saw fit, and he also technically owned all their stuff, meaning that he could send warriors to the villages to take things if he felt like doing so. A wealthy slave could purchase non slave status from the King. Slaves could also earn non slave status through labor or through service in the Zulu army. Slaves serving in the army would be organized into their own separate Impis. Since they were fighting for better status for themselves and their families, the warriors in these units were often very brave and reliable. With this being said, they were given minimal training, and when on the campaign trail they were usually just a couple of meals away from being malnourished. They were led by Zulu war chiefs who looked down upon the non Zulu men in the unit that they were placed in charge of, further decreasing unit morale and cohesion.
{zulu_spearmen_descr_short}Amabutho amaXhosa

{zulu_ww1_inf}Zulu Armoured Warriors
{zulu_ww1_inf_descr}The Zulu Armoured Warriors represent the evolution of the traditional Impi into the age of industrial warfare. Equipped with reinforced armour, trade muskets, and the iconic iklwa spear, they combine the courage and aggression of their ancestors with improved battlefield protection. Their relentless assaults can overwhelm even well-prepared defenders. They embody the enduring warrior spirit of the Zulu nation.
{zulu_ww1_inf_descr_short}Elite Zulu assault warriors combining traditional weapons with improved armour for modern battlefields.

{boer_kommando_early}Boerekommando (Early)
{boer_kommando_early_descr}Mounted citizen soldiers who dismount to fight as loose-order skirmishers. Armed with the Westley Richards Monkey Tail breech-loading rifle, they rely on fieldcraft, mobility, and accurate individual fire rather than formal line tactics.
{boer_kommando_early_descr_short}Boerekommando (Rifle-Musket, Pattern 1853 Enfield)

{boer_kommando_mid}Boerekommando (Mid)
{boer_kommando_mid_descr}Mounted citizen soldiers who dismount to fight as loose-order skirmishers. Armed with the Martini-Henry rifle, they rely on fieldcraft, mobility, and accurate individual fire rather than formal line tactics.
{boer_kommando_mid_descr_short}Boerekommando (Rifle, Martini-Henry)

{boer_kommando_high}Boerekommando (Late)
{boer_kommando_high_descr}Mounted citizen soldiers who dismount to fight as loose-order skirmishers. Armed with the Mauser Model 1895 rifle, they rely on fieldcraft, mobility, and accurate individual fire rather than formal line tactics.
{boer_kommando_high_descr_short}Boerekommando (Magazine Rifle, Mauser Model 1895)

{boer_mounted_kommando_early}Berede Boerekommando (Early)
{boer_mounted_kommando_early_descr}Mounted citizen commandos who use their horses for operational mobility and dismount when sustained fire is required. Their Pattern 1856 Enfield cavalry carbine supports reconnaissance, screening, and rapid concentration across the veld.
{boer_mounted_kommando_early_descr_short}Berede Boerekommando (Carbine, Pattern 1856 Enfield cavalry)

{boer_mounted_kommando_mid}Berede Boerekommando (Mid)
